**Context.** AdGuard Home downloads each subscribed list again every day and applies it unreviewed ([decision 46](#46-the-blocklists-are-watched-and-adguard-changes-only-on-purpose)). Its precedence is urlfilter's `IsHigherPriority`: an exception carrying `$important`, then a block carrying it, then a plain exception, then a plain block, whichever list or custom rule each sits in and in whatever order. The profile's must-allow exceptions carried `$important` ([decision 7](#7-must-allow-exceptions-are-generated-from-the-toml)), so no list could refuse them. Its blocks were plain, so a plain `@@` line in any subscribed list lifted one, LG's content-recognition operator `||alphonso.tv^` among them, and nothing in tvgw would have said so: the lint reads the profile, never the lists. A `$badfilter` line deletes the rule it names from every source, a must-allow exception included. The DoH address list had a floor of 50 entries and a ceiling, but no bound on a shrink: a source that fell from about 1,500 entries to 60 would have been taken, and every resolver it lost unblocked. And decision 46's List changes sees a list's update only once the TV asks for a name again.

**Decision.**

- **Held blocks.** A block of the `.rules` file, `||name^` or a `/regex/` with no modifier, whose name the profile files under a red-line category is loaded with `$important` (`profile.held_rule`). The red-line categories (`profile.held_category`) are content recognition, a category whose name starts with `ACR`, and the vendor's own advertising, usage reporting and fingerprinting: `<vendor> ads`, `<vendor> telemetry` and `<vendor> fingerprint`. `3rd-party ads` is not one, since a list's exception there is often an app's fix, so Nielsen's `imrworldwide.com`, comScore's `scorecardresearch.com` and the other third-party trackers stay plain; nor are voice, the IoT links, the home-screen declutter blocks or any expected category. A regex block takes the category of the first name, filled from the profile's category patterns, that it matches (`profile.regex_category`). The categories decide, so a block added to the file later is held without anyone writing the modifier; a hosts-file line, which covers no name under its own, and a rule that already carries a modifier stay as written. Every block of `roku`, `samsung-tizen` and `vizio-smartcast` is held; on `lg-webos` the ACR, LG advertising, usage-reporting and fingerprinting blocks are, and `generic-tv` has none.
- **The owner still decides.** `tvgw allow` writes `@@||name^$important` ([decision 59](#59-site-rules-etctvgwlocalrules-written-by-tvgw-allow-and-tvgw-block-applied-after-the-profile-and-before-machine-rules-under-the-same-lint)), which outranks a held block, and now says when it lifts one. The lint counts the modifiers as AdGuard does (`profile.outranks`): a plain exception over a held block lifts nothing, so it is refused as dead; a block of a must-allow name is refused with or without `$important`. "Already in place" in the analyst's vetting and the autonomy's gate treats `||name^` as the held block's twin (`profile.held_twin`), the plan does not repeat an owner's `||name^` the profile holds, and the restart guard's and the probe's blocked name may be a held one.
- **The scan.** The hourly ingest's `lists` step (`listguard.run`) reads every enabled list of AdGuard's YAML from the file AdGuard keeps it in (`data/filters/<id>.txt`, beside the query log), and never fetches anything. It runs when a list's file or the TV's rules changed, and at least once a day. It names every line that acts on one of the TV's own rules, the profile's or the owner's: `lifts`, an exception that, on at least one name the block reaches, outranks every block of the TV's that holds that name, or a `$badfilter` that deletes one; `held`, an exception the block's `$important` outranks; and `exposes`, a `$badfilter` that deletes a must-allow exception, an owner's allow or the URL filter's rewrite, an `@@...$dnsrewrite` that cancels that rewrite, or a `$dnsrewrite` of a must-allow name or of a URL-filter name, which answers before any exception. An exception is judged on the A, HTTPS and SVCB lookups it is for (`SCAN_QTYPES`; AAAA is answered empty for every client), against what the TV's own rules answer each name today: a name the owner's allow or a must-allow exception already lets through is no finding, and a plain block that a wider held block still covers is held off. A list's block of a must-allow name is no finding, since the exception outranks it. The rule that withholds HTTPS and SVCB records is not judged: a list's plain exception for a name lifts it for that name, an owner decision below. The machine's rules are left out: they come and go each hour, and the hourly check judges each one. A `lifts` or `exposes` line is a red line on the day it first appears (`a list lifts ...`, `a list can refuse what the TV needs: ...`), in the engineering brief only, since nothing at the TV or the router puts it right; every finding stays in the brief's List guard section while a list carries it, and for 90 days after (`GONE_KEEP`).
- **The preview.** When a list's file changed since the copy the step keeps (`/var/lib/tvgw/lists/<id>.txt.gz`, its rule lines), the step judges every public name the TV looked up in the 14 days before (`PREVIEW_DAYS`) against the list as it was and as it is, with every other rule as it stands, and records the names whose verdict changed in `meta.list_preview`. A preview names what the TV looked up, so it is kept 14 days (`PREVIEW_KEEP`) and `tvgw forget` removes it. It is a report, never a red line. A preview before AdGuard applies the list would need tvgw to fetch the list itself, which is decision 46's Stage B and not built; AdGuard swaps a list in as it downloads it, so the preview comes at the first hourly ingest after.
- **The model.** `listguard.Engine` is AdGuard's precedence for the rules tvgw and the lists use: `||name^`, other adblock patterns, `/regex/`, hosts-file lines, and the modifiers `$important`, `$badfilter`, `$dnsrewrite`, `$dnstype`, `$client`, `$ctag` and `$denyallow`. AdGuard drops a rule with a modifier it does not know, and so does the model; the scan counts such lines per list instead of guessing. The unit suite proves the held block against the model, and the namespace CI against the pinned binary with a list file of the test's own.
- **The DoH bound.** `tvgw-doh-update` holds a refresh back when it would drop more than `DOH_DROP_MAX` percent (default 20) of the entries of the last refresh taken: the entries no entry of the refresh covers, after today's exclusions, so an exclusion added to free a CDN edge never counts as a drop. A last refresh under the floor of 50 entries was never whole, and any refresh may replace it. Held, nothing the refresh would drop is unblocked and every entry it adds is blocked: the list in force becomes the last refresh taken plus the new entries, less what today's exclusions free, and `tvgw-apply` loads it when that changes it. The last refresh taken is kept in `/var/lib/tvgw/doh4.base` while the hold lasts, so each later refresh is judged against it, never against the grown list; `/var/lib/tvgw/doh4.held` records the hold and when that refresh was taken. `tvgw verify` warns `doh4_held`, `doh4_fresh` measures the age of the last refresh taken rather than of the file, the brief says so under Firewall held, and `sudo tvgw-doh-update --accept` takes the refresh. The default: HaGeZi's `ips/doh.txt`, the list the settings template names, dropped 1.7 to 2.1% of its entries a day over 13 versions from 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-08, after the gateway's own filtering, and 4.5% over the week (read from the repository's history on 2026-11-08). A bound of 20% is about ten days' drop in one refresh, and a week of missed refreshes is well under it.

**Consequences.**

- What is blocked today changes only where a subscribed list already carried a plain exception over a held block: that name is blocked from the deploy on, and the first scan names each such line as `held`, so the brief shows exactly which. A held exception may be a list's fix for something a newer set needs; `tvgw allow` lifts the block on purpose.
- The held blocks' rule texts change, so the first deploy's Rule changes lists each one removed and added again, AdGuard is re-applied once, in the quiet window, and `tvgw check` names the rule with its modifier.
- A list can still lift a held block with an exception that itself carries `$important`, or with a `$badfilter` naming the held rule exactly; the scan makes either a red line. Voice, IoT, declutter and third-party tracker blocks stay liftable, and each lift is a red line.
- The scan judges a regex or unanchored exception on the blocked name and one name under it, and a regex block on names filled from the categories; it does not follow CNAME targets, and neither does the preview.
- One gzipped copy of each list is kept and written when the list changes, about once a day. A scan reads every list whole, at most every hour a list changed and otherwise once a day. No schema change: three meta keys, `list_guard`, `list_preview` and `doh_hold`.
- A hold does not end on its own once the source has drifted from the last refresh taken by more than the bound, which at about 4.5% a week takes four to five weeks of an outage. So `doh4_fresh` fails 8 days after the last refresh taken, its detail says the refreshes were held, and the doctor offers to take the held refresh then. For `doh4_held` alone, a warning, the doctor offers nothing: only a person can tell a broken source from an intended change in its first week.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings or not built.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| How much of the DoH list one refresh may drop | `DOH_DROP_MAX` | `20` (percent) |
| Whether the voice, IoT or third-party tracker blocks are held too | none: `profile.held_category` | not held |
| Whether the owner's own blocks (`tvgw block`) hold against the lists | none | plain, as decision 59 wrote them |
| Whether the rule that withholds HTTPS and SVCB records from the TV carries `$important`; a list's plain exception for a name lifts it for that name | none | as before |
| Fetching and promoting list versions (decision 46's Stage B), which would let a preview come before AdGuard applies a list | none | not built |

## 69. The TV's own state, held by entries that expire, and a count of what a wall keyed to it would refuse

*2026-11-08, from research pass 5 (its item 3 and its boldest idea, attention-keyed least privilege; the internal note's sections 6.1, 6.2, 6.12 and 3.9). Built the same day, counting only: nothing new refuses traffic, `ATTENTION_WALL=refuse` is refused on a live gateway, and nothing runs where the live reading does not. The store's schema for it is 17.*

**Context.** The CX sends nothing while it is off (29 off hours, 0 connections; [decision 66](#66-an-upload-budget-with-a-ceiling-set-once-count-only-and-the-names-of-a-tv-that-goes-online-while-off)). Its idle traffic is behind the screen saver: over the 28-day evaluation, about 595 connections and 85 MB went out in hours the TV said its screen saver was on (542 connections and 83 MB under one app left behind it all night), against 7 connections and about 0.36 MB with an HDMI input or live TV in front. Those numbers come from hourly readings, so the edges of each stretch are guesses. In both states nobody is using the TV's internet side, and HDMI is where content recognition does its work: a wall that let only the TV's essentials through in those states would have real traffic to stop and almost nothing to break. No product or paper found keys a network's rules to a TV's own account of what is on its screen. [Decision 67](#67-the-tvs-own-account-of-itself-is-the-ground-sessions-a-receipt-and-a-replay-keyed-to-the-app-in-front-a-live-reading-that-never-knocks-blind-no-rule-yet) built the live reading and wrote down how such a rule would fail open: each state an element with a short lifetime that the reading renews, so a lost signal opens the wall by the kernel's clock. Two weeks of counting come before any wall may refuse ([decision 20](#20-the-gateway-blocks-on-its-own-under-three-watches) and the pass-4 notes), so this pass builds the instrument and starts the count.

**Decision.**

- **Four states from the TV's own words.** `attention.state_of` turns the live reading's power state and app into `idle` (the screen saver is on; it wins over the app or input behind it), `input` (an HDMI or other external input, `com.webos.app.hdmi*` and `com.webos.app.externalinput*`), `livetv` (`com.webos.app.livetv*`) or `app` (anything else with the TV on: an app, the home screen, a cast receiver, an id the TV chose that cannot be kept), and into none when the words do not say: no power state yet, the TV switching off, a word the vocabulary does not know, or the TV on with no app reported yet. Live TV is a state of its own because LG Channels plays inside it.
- **One kernel entry per state, renewed or gone.** `tvgw-apply` keeps four one-element sets in a table of its own, `inet tvgw_attn` (`idle4`, `input4`, `livetv4`, `app4`, each `flags timeout`). The TV's address is in the set of its state and in no other, with a lifetime of `HOLD` (8 seconds) renewed every `RENEW_EVERY` (2 seconds).
- **Two processes, so that the one facing the TV keeps no capability.** `tvgw-tvlive` holds no capability at all ([decision 67](#67-the-tvs-own-account-of-itself-is-the-ground-sessions-a-receipt-and-a-replay-keyed-to-the-app-in-front-a-live-reading-that-never-knocks-blind-no-rule-yet)), and the TV it reads was rooted from the local network in 2026 (research pass 5, external note A3). Giving it `CAP_NET_ADMIN` to write the sets would let a subverted reading rewrite the firewall. So the reading writes its word, the state and its own steady clock and nothing else (no app, no wall-clock time), to `/run/tvgw-tvlive/attention`, its `RuntimeDirectory`, whenever the state changes and every `WRITE_EVERY` (1 second) while its session's loop turns (`attention.Word`). The new service `tvgw-attention` (`tvgw attention-hold`, `attention.Holder`), bounded to `CAP_NET_ADMIN`, with netlink and unix sockets only, `IPAddressDeny=any`, the system calls of an ordinary service, no devices and no namespaces, reads that word every `POLL` (0.25 seconds), a regular file only, never through a link or from a FIFO. A word older than `FRESH` (5 seconds) is no word. It moves the TV's address in one netlink transaction, every state's set flushed and the new state's element added, so the kernel never holds the TV in two states; it renews the element the same way while the word is fresh, so each renewal is a new element with a whole lifetime, also on a kernel before 6.12, which keeps an element's first expiry when an add meets it ([decision 43](#43-the-flow-log-hears-the-tv-alone-and-the-gates-day-starts-at-each-answer)), and it computes the address from `TV_IP` and the set from a fixed list, so a subverted reading could pick only one of four states for the TV's own address. The word lives in memory: nothing is written on the SD card each second.
- **The essentials.** A fifth set, `platform4`, holds the addresses the TV needs in every state: those `appfront.classify` calls `platform` (the profile's must-allow hosts, its clock hosts and the vendor's own expected category, `LG core` on `lg-webos`). It is filled from the TV's own DNS answers, by the name the question asks for, with a day's lifetime and expiry as the gate's sets have, and by one filler only. Where `DNSGATE_FILL=inpath`, the in-path filler adds an essential's addresses before it gives the answer its verdict, in a transaction of its own after the gate's add, so a missing attention table never costs the gate its add, and a refusal by the kernel turns those adds off for `ESSENTIALS_OFF` (10 seconds). The add is ahead of the verdict on purpose: a wall can use the set only if it is filled before the TV can connect, and an add after the verdict would bring back the race the passive feeder has, as false `ESSENTIAL` samples. It costs a second netlink transaction inside the hold, bounded by the same 20 ms ([decision 64](#64-the-gates-second-set-is-filled-in-the-path-of-the-answer-beside-the-passive-feeder-and-still-only-counts)), only for an answer to an essential whose addresses the filler did not add in the last 10 seconds. Those holds are measured apart in the filler's status file (`essential_hold_us`, beside the gate's own `hold_us`), and an answer held for its essentials alone, every gate address known, is counted apart (`held_essentials`). Where `DNSGATE_FILL=passive` the passive feeder adds them, a few milliseconds after the answer, in an `nft` run of its own after the gate's, only addresses it did not add in the last 10 seconds (`dnsgate.ESSENTIALS_KNOWN`), and none for 10 seconds after a refusal (`ESSENTIALS_OFF`), so it never queues the gate's adds behind a second `nft` per answer; the report marks that race. With the in-path filler on, the passive feeder adds none: it would only widen its own race. The probe's lookups were the other candidate and are not used: they see the resolver's answer to the probe, not to the TV, every ten minutes, for the listed must-allow names only, while the TV may be handed a new address between two probes, asks for names under the listed ones, and sets its clock by names the probe never asks for. Only the TV's own answers are exact, and the in-path filler's are exact before the TV can connect.
- **The count.** In the `idle`, `input` and `livetv` states, a rule per state counts each new TCP or UDP connection of the TV to an address outside `platform4` and outside the gateway, once (`conn4` holds its tuple for two minutes, as the gate's `seen4` does), and adds it to that state's sample set, kept `SAMPLE_TIMEOUT` (3 hours). The clock's port 123 is an essential by port, LG's firmware fallback (156.147.69.32, TCP 8080) by address, and ICMP is not counted. The chain runs at `filter + 15`, after the filter's and the guard's forward chains, so it counts only what they let through; a VPN client's kill switch further on can still drop a packet it counted. Nothing is counted while an app is in front: `app4` is held so that the whole state is in the kernel, and the rule by app stays decision 67's road.
- **The samples and the counters, kept as long as the live record.** The hourly ingest copies the samples from the kernel's sets into the store's table `attention` (schema 17): when the kernel first counted the connection (the listing's time less what of its 3 hours had passed), the state, and the tuple; a sample read again in a later hour is stored once. `tvgw verify --record` stores the three rules' counters as `attention:` series. Each row says when the TV was idle or on an input, so both are pruned after `TV_EVENTS_DAYS`, as `tv_events` is, and `tvgw forget` removes both, the counters as rows of a table it otherwise keeps (`forget.ROWS_FORGET`). Nothing of this reaches the journal: the samples go nowhere but the kernel's memory and the store.
- **What the brief says.** The engineering brief gains an `Attention` section, only on a window that holds the count, so every other gateway's brief and JSON are what they were: per state, its time by the live record, the kernel's count, the samples and their bytes, and the services behind them by registrable domain (or /24), company and class, with three marks, which are what the two weeks are for: `ESSENTIAL`, an address of one of the TV's essentials the platform set did not hold (a wall would have cut the TV off from it); `just looked up`, within `FEEDER_LATE` of the answer that named it (the passive feeder's race); and `before a change`, within `LEAD_SECONDS` before the TV said the state ended (the next app starting before the TV reports it), plus the samples the live record puts in another state (the holder's lag). The plain brief gains one `Screen saver and inputs:` line naming the services a wall would have refused in the screen-saver and input states, with `Nothing was stopped: the gateway is counting.`, under the three-days-running rule of [decision 66](#66-an-upload-budget-with-a-ceiling-set-once-count-only-and-the-names-of-a-tv-that-goes-online-while-off). Both carry counts per state and service for their window and never a time of day. The time the TV spent in each state comes from the live record, so the section shows it only in an interactive brief (`tvgw brief`), never in a report `tvgw-analyze` saves, which would keep the live record's time past `TV_EVENTS_DAYS` ([decision 67](#67-the-tvs-own-account-of-itself-is-the-ground-sessions-a-receipt-and-a-replay-keyed-to-the-app-in-front-a-live-reading-that-never-knocks-blind-no-rule-yet)). The counters are kept out of Firewall held and out of the analyst's pack.
- **The switch for later, refused on a live gateway.** `ATTENTION_WALL=count|off|refuse`, default `count`, read only where `LIVE_READING=on` and the profile is `lg-webos`. `off` loads no table and runs no holder. `refuse` would add, after each of the screen-saver and input counting rules, a rule with the same match, no tuple test (a retransmission is refused again) and no counter of its own, ending in `reject with icmpx admin-prohibited`; never in live TV. `tvgw-apply` renders it only while its line `ATTENTION_REFUSE_SUPPORTED` says yes, and it says no: on a live gateway `refuse` says so in one line and counts, as `APPLY_CONFIRM=revert` acts as `report` while `REVERT_SUPPORTED=no`. The namespace tier runs a copy with the line set to yes. What the wall still lacks, each a change of its own: (1) two weeks of this count on the reference with no `ESSENTIAL` sample left unexplained; (2) the lead measured: how many seconds before the TV reports leaving the screen saver or an input the next app connects, and a grace from it; (3) the undo of a failed app start (research pass 5, item 8), so that a wall that spoils a start returns itself to counting; (4) the download condition for the screen saver (music can play behind it: the port's counters, `activity.AWAKE_BYTES`, tell a playing app from a paused one); (5) `platform4` kept across a reboot, since the TV's cache outlives it; (6) a probe that can see the table, which lives outside `inet tvgw` and so outside the probe's twin; (7) a revert that can be armed ([decision 65](#65-a-test-client-stands-where-the-tv-stands-an-apply-is-probed-before-and-after-and-only-reports-what-it-would-undo)), with the table in its last-good.
- **The connection discipline and the privacy rules of decision 67 hold.** Nothing here opens a connection to the TV: the holder reads a file, and the live reading writes one more. The word in memory is overwritten, never a record. The only new rows with a time and the TV's state are the samples, kept no longer than `tv_events`.
- **A check.** `attention` in `tvgw verify`: OK, saying so, where the count is not in use; otherwise its table's sets and rules are loaded and the holder's status is fresh. A WARN at most, never a FAIL, and its detail never names the state the TV is in.

**What happens when a part fails.** The rule: the count may stop, a wall built on it may fail to protect, and neither may hold the TV.

| What fails | What the kernel holds | What is counted |
|---|---|---|
| The live reading is off, has stood down, or has no session | No word, or a word of none: every state set empty | Nothing |
| The live reading crashes, hangs or loses the TV (a pong never comes) | Its word goes stale; within `FRESH` (5 s) the holder empties every set. A session that ends writes none at once | Nothing from then on |
| The TV is unplugged while a session holds | The session's pings go unanswered: up to 40 seconds of the last state, while the TV sends nothing | Nothing (the TV is silent) |
| The holder is killed, crashes or hangs | Nobody renews: the element expires within `HOLD` (8 s) by the kernel's clock; systemd's watchdog (5 s, SIGKILL) restarts it, and it empties every set at its start | Nothing once the element has expired |
| The holder stops cleanly | It empties every set first | Nothing |
| `tvgw-apply` runs | The table is kept (`add` statements), with its essentials and samples; only a set of other properties, found by reading each set before the load, has it made again, and a load that fails otherwise leaves it as it was. A table made again empty gets the element back at the next renewal, within 2 seconds | Up to 2 seconds of nothing after a table made again |
| The attention table is missing (an apply's gap, `ATTENTION_WALL=off` under a running holder) | Each transaction is refused (ENOENT); the holder tries again every `RETRY_EVERY` (2 s) and says so once a minute | Nothing |
| The word is not a word (too long, another version, a state outside the four, a clock that is not a number, a clock more than a second ahead) | None | Nothing |
| The essentials' add fails in the in-path filler | The answer is released as before; the address is counted when the TV connects, and marked `ESSENTIAL` in the brief | The essential's connection, marked |

**Consequences.**

- With the default settings nothing changes on any gateway: the live reading is off, so no table is loaded (`tvgw-apply` deletes one that is there, which on a gateway without one is a refused delete), the holder's condition skips it, the fillers classify nothing, the brief has no section, and the check says `not in use`. Where the live reading is on, the count starts with it, with no second switch: one table, a few rules evaluated per forwarded packet of the TV (a set lookup of the source in a one-element set), a 4 Hz read of a file in memory, a transaction every 2 seconds, and, by whichever filler runs, one more netlink transaction inside the hold (the in-path filler) or one more `nft` run after the gate's (the passive feeder) per answer to an essential whose addresses it did not add in the last 10 seconds.
- The count trusts the TV's word, as every state-keyed rule does (research pass 5, internal note 4.11): a TV that said `app` while showing its screen saver would not be counted, and one that stopped reporting reads as none. It protects against apps and features the vendor admits to, not against a vendor willing to lie on its own local interface.
- An address that is an essential's and also a tracker's (a shared delivery network) is not counted, the gate's own limit. A certificate check or a delivery network the profile files under `Infra` is counted, since it is not an essential by this rule; whether a wall must let certificate checks through in these states is for the two weeks to show.
- Music or a video left playing behind the screen saver is counted in `idle`, and a wall would stop it: the download condition, item (4) above.
- The namespace tier's ruleset check now renders every variant with `LIVE_READING` on and off, twice as many as before; the comparison of `DNSGATE_FILL=passive` with a file without the key runs on the half with the live reading off.
- `ESSENTIAL` samples on the first day after a reboot are expected: `platform4` is empty while the TV's cache is not, as for the gate's sets.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| Whether the attention count runs where the live reading does | `ATTENTION_WALL` | `count` |
| Whether a wall refuses in the screen-saver and input states | `ATTENTION_WALL=refuse`, refused on a live gateway while `ATTENTION_REFUSE_SUPPORTED=no` in the script | counts |
| Whether certificate checks and shared delivery networks count among the essentials | no setting: a change of code, after the two weeks | not essentials |

## 70. Each TLS connection is checked against the names the filter answered with its address: a report from the store, a live count from copies, and nothing refused

*2026-11-08, from research pass 5 (its external note's B1 and section E, its internal note's 3.2 and 4.1 to 4.6; build package C). Built count-only: the report reads the store and changes nothing; the live count runs only once the owner sets `NAME_BINDING=count`. The store's schema for it is 15.*

**Context.** A DNS filter decides which names the TV may look up, not which servers it may talk to. The address gate makes the filter's word count at the level of addresses: it counts connections to an address no answer gave ([decision 18](#18-dns-gated-egress-starts-as-a-count-in-its-own-table), [decision 64](#64-the-gates-second-set-is-filled-in-the-path-of-the-answer-beside-the-passive-feeder-and-still-only-counts)). On a shared cloud server one address serves thousands of names, and the server picks the site from the name in the TLS hello. So a connection whose hello names something the filter never answered with that address, a tracker reached through the address of an allowed name on the same CDN, passes the gate unseen. The research found no product or paper that binds the hello's name to the same client's own answers without decrypting, and found every part of it already in tvgw: the captured hello ([decisions 33](#33-parse-the-held-back-first-segment-of-a-split-hello), [34](#34-a-hello-without-a-name-gets-its-own-table) and [36](#36-a-known-ech-public-name-is-real-ech)), the answers, and the plumbing beside the packet path. Research passes 2 and 4 also asked for a table of the addresses each answer gave, so that a connection without a hello can be named by its lookups, with one guard: a name found that way never feeds what decides as if the TV's packets had carried it ([decision 12](#12-each-flow-is-named-by-its-own-tls-hello)). The store kept each answer only as text, matched with `LIKE`, and without its lifetime.

**Decision.**

1. **The record.** `answer_ip` (schema 15): one row per public address an A answer to the TV named, keyed to its `dns` row, with the answer's time and the smallest lifetime of its A records ([the table](reference/data-model.md#answer_ip)). The names stay in `dns`, so the name keys and `tvgw forget` reach them where they always did. The hourly ingest writes the rows of each new answer, turns the answers stored before the table into rows once (newest first, `BACKFILL_ROWS = 50_000` lookups an hour, back `ANSWER_IP_DAYS = 35` days, their lifetime unknown) and prunes at 35 days, or at `DB_DAYS` when that is shorter. The table records from when it covers every answer (`meta.answer_ip_since`). The in-path filler is not a source: its reader decodes no name by design (decision 64's linear reader), so it cannot say which name an address was answered for.
2. **Stage 1, a report that changes nothing.** `tvgw.namebound` puts each TLS connection the capture saw, over TCP to a public address that is not the gateway's, in one of five classes: **name-bound** (the hello's name is a name the filter answered with that address in the day before it: the question or any name of the answer's CNAME chain); **shared address, other name** (the address was answered in that day, but only for other names: the shared-CDN case the gate cannot see); **never answered** (the gate's own case); **no name** (`tls_noname`); **ECH** (`queries.ech_seen`'s test). The day is the gate's (`dnsgate.TTL`), not the record's lifetime: the TV's cache, an app's cache and the gate's set all outlive it. A hello may come at most `SLACK = 1.0` second before the time AdGuard logged its answer. A hello carried over QUIC is left out, since the firewall refuses QUIC. A connection whose day before reaches past the table's coverage is **not assessed** unless the table already shows it name-bound, never "shared" or "never answered": an answer the table never saw may explain it. The engineering brief's new section counts the classes and lists the connections of the middle two by name, address and port, each with what explains it: the names its address was answered for, a name of the same site, a name the filter blocked or answered with other addresses, an older answer that gave the name this address, a must-allow name. The plain brief adds one line, `Names:` ("under a name the gateway had not given out for that server in the day before"), on a day with a connection of the middle class: count-only, never an action, and left out after `REPEAT_DAYS` days running with no new service, as `While off:` and `Uploads:` are. `tvgw host NAME` shows a name's connections by class; `tvgw host ADDRESS` lists the answers that gave the address and the names the TV used there.
3. **Naming the connections a hello did not name, with a guard.** A flow with no hello is named by the newest question answered with its address in the day before it began, only when every question answered with that address there sits under one registrable domain (`SHARED_FROM = 2`: an address answered for two services names neither). Such a name is labelled `by its answers`, appears only in this section, and never reaches the categories, the upload budget, the analyst's pack or the autonomy; a test holds the pack to it. A persisted report (the daily files `tvgw-analyze` writes, `root:adm 640`) carries the section's names and counts but no time of any connection: the samples' first and last times and the time of an older answer stay in the store and in the interactive brief, as decision 67 keeps the finer record of the app in front.
4. **The meters that fall out of it.** The middle class, by destination, is the server-name mismatch meter. The DoH-shaped connections are those to TCP 443 longer than 10 minutes with over 500 packets each way averaging under 300 bytes, the research note's third measure of a resolver tunnel; the DoH wall resets port 443 to every listed resolver address, so each one reached an address the list does not hold, and the section says when its name's lookups were blocked. The shares of the TV's bytes up named by a hello, and by a single service's answers alone, are printed in the section, and the plain brief's TV line gains one clause that keeps the two apart, "The gateway could read the server's name for N% of what it sent, and match M% more to the TV's lookups", each rounded down, and left out on a day with a flow the record could not assess.
5. **Stage 2, a live count from copies.** A new setting, `NAME_BINDING=off|count`, default `off`. With `count`, `tvgw-apply` loads the table `inet tvgw_names` ([its rules](../deploy/nftables/README.md#the-names-table)): two base chains with policy accept and no verdict, whose rules copy with `log group 8` (NFLOG) every DNS answer the gateway sends the TV (output hook, one priority after the in-path filler's queue) and the first 8 packets the TV sends on each TCP connection to port 443 (forward hook, after every chain that can refuse, so only connections the filter let through). `tvgw-names` (`src/tvgw/nametap.py`) binds the group, remembers for the gate's day which names each answer gave each address (at most 65,536 addresses of 64 names each), reads the name from each connection's first hello, assembling a split one from at most six segments, and counts it in stage 1's classes. It fills its memory at start from AdGuard Home's query log, the TV's answers of the last day. Its status file, root-only, keeps its last five mismatches by name, address and port; with `NAME_HMAC=on` the names there are tagged as the store tags them, and one whose key the sandboxed process cannot read is left out. `tvgw verify --record` stores its counts as series under the prefix `names:`, kept out of "Firewall held", and the brief's section adds one line with them, so the live count and the store's report can be compared. A new check, `names_tap`, is OK and says `not in use` with the default; with `count` it checks the table, the bound group and a fresh status, and is a WARN at most, always (`FAIL_AFTER` says WARN): nothing waits for this process.

**Why copies, and not the queue the research proposed.** The external note's stage 2 queued the first data packet of each new connection to netfilter's packet queue, with `bypass`, and always accepted. A copy is better on every count that matters here. It holds no packet: the kernel copies it and the packet goes on, so a reader that is slow, stopped, killed or missing costs the TV nothing, and decision 64's failure table collapses to one row. The queue's admitted losses would apply to the TV's hellos: a queue whose socket closes, and every queue when any hook is removed, drops what waits (`nfqnl_flush`). And the copy keeps the order that keeps the gate's race out: the answer and the hello go to one group, so to one socket, in the order the kernel copied them; an answer is copied on its way out of the gateway and the TV cannot send a hello for it before it arrives, so the reader always meets the answer first, however late it reads. The passive DNS-gate feeder races because it adds an address to a set the kernel checks at the same moment; this count checks nothing in the kernel. nftables cannot read a TLS name, so no set of names and addresses can do this in the kernel.

**What the live count cannot vouch for, each counted with its reason.** A connection that would count as `shared` or `never` is not assessed instead when the memory cannot vouch for it, and the series say why. *A copy lost or an answer not read* (`LOSS_PROTECT = 3600` seconds in `src/tvgw/nametap.py`): when the socket's buffer is full the kernel drops a copy and the next read says so (ENOBUFS), and an answer can arrive in a form the reader does not read (a TCP answer across segments, one over 4,096 bytes); either may have been the answer a later hello follows, so for the hour after it every mismatch is not assessed. An hour is longer than the lifetime the TV's answers mostly carry and shorter than the day an app's own cache can reach, so a loss can still make a later connection count `never`: which is why the losses are counted beside it. *The memory short of a day*: its first read of AdGuard Home's query log did not reach back a day, or, for `STARTUP_GRACE = 900` seconds after start, the address was shown by no live copy, since AdGuard Home writes its log in batches and the newest lookups are missing from that read. *An address of too many names*: a name is let go to make room for a new one only while its day lasts (names whose day is over go first), and the address counts as crowded until that name's day would have ended.

**Stage 3, designed and not built.** Refusing a connection whose name the filter never answered with its address needs a verdict, so the hello must be held: netfilter's queue on the first data packet to port 443, with `bypass` and fail-open, marked so a connection is queued once. The memory and the verdict must be in one process that has read every answer before the hello it judges, so the answers must reach the same queue, in the order the copies keep now. A userspace verdict cannot send a reset; the verdict would set a mark and repeat the packet, and a rule matching the mark would `reject with tcp reset`, so an app retries at once rather than waiting for a silent drop. The rule must match only while the process renews a set element with a short timeout, so a dead reader turns refusal off by the kernel's clock (the construction the internal note's 2.2 and decision 67 name). Must-allow names (the clock, LG sign-in, the store, firmware) are exempt, or bound only by registrable domain; the Content Store and the GFTS URL filter pass through the gateway's own nginx and are not affected. It may refuse only after the two-week counting rule ([decision 20](#20-the-gateway-blocks-on-its-own-under-three-watches) and pass 4's notes): fourteen days of stage 2 with `lost` at 0 and the middle two classes at a handful a week, each explained, and with decision 65's revert armable. What stays open even then: ECH (the red line), TLS without a name (counted), non-TLS traffic (the gate and the unusual ports), and fronting inside the encrypted request, which no gateway can see.

**Consequences.** The store gains one table and two indexes; the hourly ingest inserts a lookup at a time to key its rows, and writes one row per address of each allowed answer. The brief runs one indexed read of the answers per address the TV reached, and the plain line's streak reads up to three days more. On the CX the middle class is expected near zero, as the gate found two genuine misses in five days; its value is insurance on the shared CDNs. Nothing was measured on the reference before this change: the read-only commands in the hand-back measure the first 28 days once the backfill has run. With `NAME_BINDING=off` an apply renders every table as before, `settings.applied` gains `NAME_BINDING=off`, an apply runs no nft command it did not run before (only the first apply after one with `count` deletes the table, as `settings.applied` says it was loaded), verify gains one OK check, and `tvgw-names.service` is copied and left disabled. With `count`: one more root service with `CAP_NET_ADMIN` only, no network or packet socket and a read-only system, and a copy of each DNS answer and of up to 8 packets of each TLS connection, a few thousand a day on the CX. The kernel's half (the copies, their order on one socket, a reader that dies) is proven only in the namespace tier and on the gateway.

**Not in this change.** Refusing anything (stage 3). The in-path filler reading names. The internal note's DNS-starvation ratio and must-allow upload ledger (its 4.5 and 4.6): the first needs a baseline the brief does not keep, the second belongs with the upload budget, and both are left for a later pass.

## 71. The port walls are ready to switch, the probe checks them, and tvgw refused reads the firewall

*2026-11-08, from research pass 5 (its internal note's sections 2.3, 2.4 and 2.14, and its build items 4 and 5). Built the same day. Nothing new refuses traffic: with the settings as they ship, every ruleset `tvgw-apply` loads is byte for byte what it loaded before, and the LG profile keeps the clock port open.*

**Context.** Two walls of the firewall were left open on purpose until evidence existed. `STRICT_PORTS=yes` (decision 17) refuses every port but the web, the clock and DNS; its condition is two clean weeks of "Unexpected ports", and the odd-port record goes back to late October. The clock port, UDP 123, was the widest path left open by design: since 2026-11-07 the gateway counts the TV's NTP by server (`inet tvgw_ntp`), and on webOS 5 the clock comes from LG's SDP reply, so the count is expected to stay at zero; its two weeks end about 21 November on the reference. Neither wall could be switched on safely the day its evidence is in, for three reasons. Nothing said in one line whether the two weeks were clean. The probe from the TV's position (decision 65) exercised no port, so a confirmed apply could not see a strict wall that let everything through, or one that refused the web (decision 65's fifth missing piece, for the ports). And `tvgw refused`, the triage that makes a wall livable, read AdGuard Home's log alone, so every refusal the kernel makes was invisible to it.

**Decision.**

- **The clock port is a profile key with a setting over it.** The profile's `[firewall]` table takes `ntp = "any" | "resolved" | "none"`, default `any` (`profile.NTP_POLICIES`): a TV's clock is vendor knowledge, and Android TV, Samsung and Roku sets use NTP. `CLOCK_PORT` in the settings file, default `profile`, follows the profile, or says `any`, `resolved` or `none` for one gateway, which is also the owner's undo that needs no new package. `tvgw-apply` reads the profile's key through `tvgw profile --firewall`, the one reader of the TOML, and gives it 10 seconds; a profile it cannot read in that time leaves the port open and says so. It records `CLOCK_PORT` (which verify's `applied_current` compares), what the profile said (`CLOCK_PORT_PROFILE`, `unread` when it could not ask) and what it built (`CLOCK_PORT_USED`) in `settings.applied`, which a restore copies back with the rules.
- **`none` closes the port in `tv_out`**, ahead of the odd-port rules: a log sample (`tvgw-ntp:`, six a minute) and `udp dport 123 counter reject`, comment `clock port: closed, NTP to no server`, a Firewall held line of its own (`clock port`). In `tv_out`, the probe's twin carries it with no further work. The two rules are rendered on the line of the odd-port log rule, so a ruleset without them is unchanged byte for byte.
- **`resolved` refuses NTP to an address no lookup returned**, in the gate's table, since only there are the sets it reads: a chain `clock` at forward priority filter - 3, in a load of its own after the in-path block that makes `resolved4_inpath`. An address either gate set holds (the passive feeder's too, so a filler that died costs the first request to a new server, never the clock) enters `ntp_ok4`, a dynamic set with a 10-day timeout that each request renews, as the NTP count keeps its servers; NTP to an address none of the three holds is sampled and refused, comment `clock port: only to an address a lookup returned`. An address once admitted stays admitted while the TV keeps asking it, though the gate forgets an address a day after its lookup: NTP clients poll one address for days after one lookup. The probe's port crosses none of the gate's table, so the probe can only see that the port is not refused.
- **A narrowing is held back on a live gateway where it could cut the TV's clock off**, and the apply says so and builds the port open. `none`: while the TV's NTP shows in either of two readings, since neither is whole alone: the NTP count's set in the kernel (a server the TV asked in the count's 10 days, up to this minute, but emptied by every reboot), and the record of that count in the store (`tvgw ports --ntp-seen`, the TV's requests over the last 14 days, which a reboot keeps; the review of 2026-11-08 found that the kernel's set alone would let a power cut after an owner's `none` close the port though the TV had used NTP the day before); and while that record cannot be read. Both are read before the confirmation arms its restore timer. The count sits after every chain that can refuse, so once the port is closed it counts nothing, and the closing rule's counter and samples are the evidence from then on; a gateway with no store yet has no record, and the narrowing stands as asked if the live count is empty too. `resolved`: always for now, by a line of the script, `CLOCK_RESOLVED_SUPPORTED=no`, as `REVERT_SUPPORTED`; and anywhere without `DNSGATE=count` and `DNSGATE_FILL=inpath`. What `resolved` lacks before that line may say yes: (1) the in-path filler's own two weeks of count (decision 64); (2) an alive switch with an expiry for the filler, so that a filler killed outright is known (pass 5's seam 3); (3) the gate's sets kept across a reboot, since a reboot empties them and a TV still using a server it looked up before would be refused until it looks up again; and (4) a restore that puts the gate's table back with the rest (decision 65's sixth missing piece), since the rule lives there. The namespace tier runs copies of the script with the line set to yes.
- **The probe's port steps.** At its documentation address (RFC 5737), all at once, in a second and a half, the probe tries TCP 80 and 443, UDP 123, and one unusual port, UDP 5000 (`probe.ODD_PORT`, in none of the walls' lists). A port the TV uses that the gateway refuses is a `fail` whatever the uplink, since the refusal is the gateway's own; a port that must be refused and is let through is a `fail` with the uplink up and `unknown` without it, as for the walls. UDP 123 must be refused exactly when the last apply built the port closed, and UDP 5000 exactly when `STRICT_PORTS=yes`, both as `settings.applied` records them. The unusual port is UDP so that the refusal can be read whole: the client reads the ICMP error from the socket's error queue (`IP_RECVERR`), and a TCP try's from a raw ICMP socket (the client is root in its namespace). Only the gateway's own error of a refusing kind is a refusal: destination unreachable with code 3 (port, what `reject` sends) or 9, 10 or 13 (administratively prohibited). Any other error from the gateway is a route it lacks: when the Wi-Fi drops in direct mode, or the WAN in vpn mode, the default routes go and the gateway answers net unreachable from `GW_IP` itself, which the review of 2026-11-08 found would have read as a refused clock port and failed the probe. Such an error is `unknown`, never a fail and never a pass, and so is one the probe cannot attribute (a TCP try the raw socket did not see). An error from the home router or the provider means the try left the gateway. When `net.ipv4.icmp_errors_use_inbound_ifaddr` is not 0 the gateway's errors carry another address than `GW_IP`, so a refusal could pass as an error from beyond: the step then says it cannot tell. A datagram not answered is sent again after 1.05 seconds, past the kernel's per-destination ICMP limit (a burst of six, then one a second), so the walls' refusals and these cannot leave an expected refusal silent. The new stage is `ports`, in the verdict's table, in the sample `tvgw verify --record` stores, and in every confirmed apply. "The vendor's" ports are not tried: the TV's one vendor port, LG's firmware fallback on 8080, is accepted only to LG's own address, and the probe never contacts the vendor. The adtech wall's ports are refused before any of these and are pass 5's revert package's to probe.
- **`tvgw refused` reads the firewall too** (`src/tvgw/walls.py`). The counters of the refusing rules of `inet tvgw` and of the clock chain in `inet tvgw_gate`, live, less the store's newest snapshot at or before the window (up to ten minutes earlier; each line says from when it counts), stepped through the snapshots and the apply's own listing pair after it as `queries.fw_activity` steps them; with no snapshot in the hour before, from the last apply's listing, and with neither, no count and a note. And the samples its rules log to the kernel journal (`tvgw-oddport`, `tvgw-ntp`, `tvgw-adport`, and `tvgw-gate` once the gate's settings refuse), which the store has only after the hourly ingest: they name an unusual port's or a clock server's address, named in turn from the TV's own answers and the company snapshot. An unusual port's samples are refusals only while the strict reject is loaded. Each refusal is ranked with the refused names by the same key: the clock port, once narrowed, as a name the TV needs; an unusual port, an address on the owner's own block list, or one the gate refused, when the record has not seen it refused before (an unusual port the TV used while it was let through, and that `STRICT_PORTS=yes` refuses now, is a change, and new); the walls kept on purpose (QUIC, encrypted lookups, the home network, LG's advertising ports, tunnels, large pings, the gateway's own services) after every name, never a likely cause, since an app falls back from them or they are the isolation itself. Structural drops (a forged source, the wrong uplink) are `tvgw doctor`'s. A wall whose rule was taken away in the window keeps what its counter counted before; and when AdGuard Home's log cannot be read, the firewall's half is shown on its own under the line that says why, since it does not depend on the log and may be the cause. No new log rule was added to the existing walls: their counters are exact, and a sample of every QUIC attempt would grow the journal on every gateway.
- **`tvgw ports`: the two weeks, in one line a wall.** From the store, read-only: for `STRICT_PORTS=yes`, the odd-port rule's count over the last 14 days and its samples by destination and port; for the clock port, the NTP count's servers, each with the names the TV's answers gave it. Ready means the count ran 14 days (`ports.READY_DAYS`), counted nothing, and the TV was online on at least 7 of them (`ONLINE_DAYS`), so that a clean count is not a TV that was off. Decision 17 also accepts entries "looked up and accepted"; the reading cannot know what the owner accepted, so any entry blocks it and is listed for the owner to judge. Servers that each came from a lookup point at `resolved`; one no lookup named blocks both narrowings. Once a wall is on, the same command is its probation report: what it refused in the last 14 days. A command and not a brief line, because the decision is taken once, when the owner chooses, over a window the daily brief does not read; the brief's daily lines are the evidence, and `ports.strict_readiness()` and `ports.clock_readiness()` are there for a brief line or a probation hook to call.
- **A refusing clock port is said, not counted as success.** With the port closed, the TV's NTP shows in Firewall held only as `clock port N`, beside the walls that held, while the Clock servers line reads nothing and the probe passes by design. So the engineering brief adds a line saying what the refusals may cost (the TV's clock may drift, and sign-in and paid video can then fail) and how to open the port, and the plain brief one `Clock:` line, never an action (review of 2026-11-08).
- **Probation and undo are what exists.** The switch is a setting and an apply; with `TV_PROBE=on` and `APPLY_CONFIRM=report` the apply is probed before and after, and now the probe checks the ports. The undo is the setting back and an apply. `tvgw refused` names what the wall refuses, the likely cause first. No second undo is built: an automatic one waits for `REVERT_SUPPORTED=yes` (decision 65) or a failed-start signal (pass 5's "did it work?" package).

**Consequences.**

- On the reference, nothing changes at deploy: `CLOCK_PORT` is absent (so `profile`, and the LG profile says `any`), `STRICT_PORTS=no`, and the probe is off. Each apply now runs `tvgw profile --firewall`, a few tenths of a second of Python, and records three more lines in `settings.applied`. The first decisions this enables: `STRICT_PORTS=yes` on probation as soon as `tvgw ports` says ready (the record already covers more than two weeks), and `CLOCK_PORT=none`, or the LG profile's `ntp = "none"`, after about 21 November if the NTP count stays at zero.
- A clock port closed on a TV that starts using NTP after a firmware update: the count sees nothing, but the closing rule counts and samples each request, `tvgw refused` ranks it first with the fix, and `tvgw ports` reports it. The interlock does not reopen the port by itself; that is the owner's undo, or a later automatic one.
- The probe adds well under half a second to each run, and up to a second and a half when a port must be refused (it waits for that refusal, and sends again past the kernel's ICMP limit), and up to two ICMP errors to its address (the strict refusal and the closed clock port) beside the two its walls already draw. Its port tries that the gateway lets through leave with a time to live of two (`probe.PORT_TTL`): they die at the next hop, the home router or the VPN's server, which answers at once with a time-exceeded, so nothing of them goes further, and no error from beyond arrives after the probe's port is gone (the namespace tier found one such late error turning into a neighbour entry for the probe's address on the TV's port).
- `tvgw refused` now needs `nft` and the journal, both root's, as it already was; on a computer without nft it reports as before. A count that reaches back to the snapshot before the window says so on its line.
- `ntp = "resolved"` is rendered and tested on a kernel, and switched on nowhere. The in-path half's clean-up before the loads now also takes the `clock` chain (a hook, so while the queue is quiet) and, unless the apply builds `resolved` again, its set.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| Whether the TV may send NTP to any server, to servers its lookups named, or to none | `CLOCK_PORT`, and the profile's `[firewall] ntp` | `profile`, and `any` in every bundled profile; `none` held back while the TV used NTP in the last 10 days; `resolved` held back on every live gateway |
| Whether unusual ports are refused | `STRICT_PORTS` (decision 17) | `no`; `tvgw ports` says when the two weeks are clean |

## 72. Did it work: a failed app start is read from the TV's own account and joined with what was refused in its minutes; a wall on probation asks one question; what is counting, since when, and when its two weeks end

*2026-11-08, from research pass 5 (its internal note's sections 5.1, item 8, and 6.5, and ranked items 1 and 7). Built: the failed starts in `tvgw receipt`, the brief and the plain brief; `didit.since`, the probation hook, with no caller yet but its tests; the runs of the count-only mechanisms, kept by `tvgw verify --record`, in the brief, the plain brief and `tvgw status`. Nothing here refuses, changes or sends anything.*

**Context.** A household's first question after any new wall is "did the gateway break Netflix?", and nothing in the brief answered it: no line said that an app failed to start, and none tied a failure to something the gateway refused. Every wall research pass 5 builds also needs an automatic undo, and decision 67 already names its trigger, "an app goes back to the home screen within a minute of starting twice in an evening"; nothing computed it. Separately, every new wall counts for two weeks before it may refuse ([design decision 17](#17-strict_ports-stays-off-until-two-clean-weeks)'s two clean weeks, which [decision 20](#20-the-gateway-blocks-on-its-own-under-three-watches) and research pass 4 kept), and five mechanisms now count (the NTP count, the upload budget of [decision 66](#66-an-upload-budget-with-a-ceiling-set-once-count-only-and-the-names-of-a-tv-that-goes-online-while-off), the in-path filler of [decision 64](#64-the-gates-second-set-is-filled-in-the-path-of-the-answer-beside-the-passive-feeder-and-still-only-counts), the probe of [decision 65](#65-a-test-client-stands-where-the-tv-stands-an-apply-is-probed-before-and-after-and-only-reports-what-it-would-undo) and the live reading of [decision 67](#67-the-tvs-own-account-of-itself-is-the-ground-sessions-a-receipt-and-a-replay-keyed-to-the-app-in-front-a-live-reading-that-never-knocks-blind-no-rule-yet)), but nothing said which were counting, since when, or when the first decision falls due. The research found no product or paper that joins a TV's own report of the app in front with what a gateway refused in the same minutes: quality-of-experience inference exists in operators' networks, from traffic alone.

**Decision.**

- **Only an app the gateway has seen working is judged, and only a start the TV reported.** `src/tvgw/didit.py` reads the sessions `appfront.timeline()` cuts. An app is judged only when it has a picture as of that viewing day (a site of its own reached on an earlier day, as the receipt learns it), is of the kind `app` (never the home screen, an input or a cast receiver) and is not one of the TV maker's own (`SYSTEM_APPS`: settings, the input picker, the media player and the like, which bring nothing down when they work). A live session of such an app whose start and end the TV reported to the second, that began with a change of app and ended with one to the home screen, is a candidate when it brought under `LITTLE_DOWN` (1 MiB) down on the connections that started in it: *quick* when in front under `SHORT` (60 seconds), *stalled* when up to `STALL_MAX` (600 seconds). Quick exits of one app, each within `CLUSTER` (600 seconds) of the one before, are one episode; a stall is an episode of its own. A start that ended at another app, an input, a cast receiver or standby is a choice; one the gateway did not see begin (the first app a session to the TV reports, or one the TV woke with) or lost touch during is its own blind spot; neither is ever counted. `LITTLE_DOWN` is a guess, as `appfront.LEAD_SECONDS` is, until the live record has measured starts that worked and starts that did not.
- **Nothing is judged before its bytes settle.** conntrack logs a connection when it lets it go, up to `receipt.SETTLE` later, and until then a start that worked reads as one that failed. A candidate whose bytes have not settled is pending, never a failed start, so a wall's first refusal of a name cannot turn a working start into a failure.
- **What is shown.** `REPEAT` (2) quick exits in one episode are a failed start on their timing alone, but only for a fresh start: an app that was in front earlier that viewing day can come back from memory, bringing little down, and its quick exits look the same. Everything else, one quick exit, a stall, the quick exits of an app back from memory, is shown only with a likely cause in its minutes. From the hourly readings alone, one pair of readings a short hour apart (awake, naming the same app, not behind the screen saver) that brought under `HOURLY_LITTLE_DOWN` (10 MiB) down is shown only with a strong cause between the two readings, since a window of up to an hour makes a coincidence likelier, and its line says that its times are to the hour and that an app left on its menu reads the same: that is the lower confidence the readings allow.
- **A likely cause is judged as `tvgw refused` judges a name.** The store's refused lookups in the episode's minutes (`SLACK`, 5 seconds, either side of its stays; record types the TV goes without left out) are each a cause, judged with `refused.needed`, `refused.store_history` and `refused.domain_history`, and so is each refusal another reader passes as a `didit.Refusal` row (a kernel refusal a wall reads), judged by its own `seen_before`, since only that reader knows its history. The strong causes are a name the profile says the TV needs and a name whose site the app reached on earlier viewing days; a likely cause is a strong one, or a refusal its record has not seen before (by its rule under its site, or after the filter had let the name through). None is ever a known tracker (`plain.is_tracking`), refused on purpose, as `tvgw refused` never ranks one as the cause. A refusal in the same minutes is a match in time: no line says it caused anything; each says what refused it (the filter, or the gateway for another reader's refusal) and ends with the command that says why (`sudo tvgw check NAME`, or `sudo tvgw refused`), and the receipt's forbidden words hold for these lines too. The fix is that command's, not repeated here, since a privacy gateway does not print an unblock for what it refuses on purpose.
- **Where it is shown.** `tvgw receipt` opens with the failed starts of its window (the evening's on the default screen), one line each: "Netflix opened 2 times from 20:14, and each time the TV went back to the home screen within 18 seconds (39.1 KB down); in those minutes the filter refused api.example.net (other services), which it had not refused before: sudo tvgw check api.example.net". The interactive `tvgw brief` carries the same lines in a section "Did it work", and its plain form one `Apps:` line; the brief judges with the same pictures the receipt has, so the two never disagree. A screen with none says nothing: a start that stayed is no proof that it played, so no line says everything worked.
- **Nothing minute-level in a persisted report.** A report the gateway writes (`tvgw report`, the daily and weekly files, the JSON beside them) carries `failed_starts` as counts alone: how many, how many with a cause that counts for it, how many from the hourly readings. Its line names no app, no time and no name, and points at `sudo tvgw receipt --since <day>`. The lines with an app, a time and a name are computed only for an interactive run, as the receipt is, so decision 67's rule holds: the minute-level record does not outlive `TV_EVENTS_DAYS` in `reports/`. A daily count of failed starts says no more about the household than the brief's hours on. None of it is in the analyst's pack, and none of it is an action: `action.txt` and the login message are what they were.
- **The probation hook.** `didit.since(conn, profile, t, now)` answers "did anything break since `t`?" with a `Verdict`: `broke` with the failed starts and their likely causes ranked (`Verdict.causes`); `clean` only when none failed, none is pending, at least one app start's settled bytes show it working (in front `SHORT` or longer with `LITTLE_DOWN` down, or an hourly session with `HOURLY_LITTLE_DOWN` down: time in front alone proves nothing) and the record of lookups (`covered_until`) reaches past the end of the last app stay since `t`; `unknown` otherwise, which is never `clean`. It reads the store and the apps' pictures; the hourly ingest fills the store up to an hour late, so a caller that must answer sooner passes the live reading's own lines (`events`) and the refusals the store does not hold yet (`extra`). A wall's undo acts on `broke` only for causes it can tie to its own refusals. No wall calls it yet.
- **What is counting.** `src/tvgw/counting.py` lists the count-only mechanisms (`MECHANISMS`): the NTP count, the upload budget, the in-path filler, the probe and the live reading; a later count adds one entry (the attention counts of research pass 5, when they land). Every `tvgw verify --record` run looks at what its own run shows of each: the NTP count's set row and the in-path filler's miss rule among the counters it stored (the filler only with `DNSGATE_FILL=inpath`), a probe verdict, `LIVE_READING=on` for the LG profile with no stand-down marker, a ceiling the budget counts against. It keeps, in the store's meta under `counting`, when each one's current run began and when it was last seen: times of the gateway's own runs, never of anything the TV did. `RUN_GAP` (12 hours) with nothing showing a mechanism breaks its run, so every calendar day of a run has counted, and a stand-down of the live reading breaks its run at once; the next sight begins a new one. The first time, a mechanism takes its start from its own record (the first snapshot of its current run in `fw_counters` or `gw_samples`, the time the budget's ceiling was set), except the live reading: its record is the TV's own events, whose times say when the TV was watched, so its run starts at the first record that sees it on and is never read from `tv_events`. The budget's run is its ceiling's, from `set_t`, while the gateway's own ceiling is in use. Two weeks after its start (`TWO_WEEKS`) a mechanism has reached them.
- **Two weeks are judged where the record can, and only there.** The NTP count is clean with no request in its two weeks and the upload budget with no hour over its ceiling, each only when the TV was on for `MIN_ON_HOURS` (14) clock hours of them, by its hourly readings or, where nothing reads it, the TV port's counters: a TV left off asks no time server and uploads nothing. The probe is clean with no failed test, no apply it would have undone and a passing run on every day; a run that could not judge is no evidence either way. The in-path count is the owner's to judge, since each miss needs a cause, and the live reading's is decision 67's bar, which `tvgw receipt --replay` reports: those two say that they have counted two weeks and where the numbers are, and never that they are clean.
- **Where it is shown, by the day.** The engineering brief gains a section "Counting", one line a mechanism (off with the setting that starts it, waiting, stopped, counting since a date with the date its two weeks end, or reached with what they hold), printed once one counts or has counted. The plain brief gains one `Counting:` line for a mechanism whose two weeks ended in its window, for three days (the plain brief's `REPEAT_DAYS`), saying what the two weeks hold and what the owner may now decide, and that nothing changes unless the owner decides it: "The clock-server count has two clean weeks, since 7 November: the TV asked no time server. So the clock port could be closed for this TV. Nothing changes unless you decide it." `tvgw verify --record` leaves the runs in `counting.txt` beside the status file, mode 644, one line a mechanism (its key, its state and two dates, no detail), and `tvgw status`, which reads no store and no settings, adds a heading and one line for each mechanism that counts after its three lines, or one line saying the file is stale when it is over 30 minutes old. Every time a report or that file carries is a date: no time of day leaves the store.

**Consequences.** Nothing is refused, changed or sent: the failed starts and the runs are reports, and a count that reaches two weeks changes nothing on its own; whether a wall then refuses stays the owner's decision. The live reading's record is read as decision 67 keeps it, and no new record of the household is made: the meta row holds the times of the gateway's own runs. A failed start is a coincidence of a short stay, little download and, at most, a refusal; an app that shows its own error for a minute while its service is down reads as one, and the line then says that the gateway refused nothing in those minutes, which points away from the gateway. An app the gateway has never seen working, a newly installed one, is not judged at all, so its failure is not shown. A lookup the TV answered from its own cache of a refusal (60 seconds, the blocked answer's TTL) leaves no refused lookup in the minutes, so a cause can be missed. Over the hourly readings alone a start is seen only as a pair of readings that brought little down, so a failed start under about two hours is not seen. The persisted brief gains a section and a JSON key only on a day with a failed start, and the Counting section on a gateway where something counts, which on the reference is every day, since the NTP count runs with the firewall; the plain brief gains a line only for a failed start or a count that reached its two weeks. `tvgw status` prints more than three lines once something counts. The probation hook's verdict is only as fresh as what it is given: from the store alone, up to an hour old, and `unknown` for up to `receipt.SETTLE` after the last start.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.** None: every part reads what the gateway already records, and what a persisted report may carry is fixed by decision 67.

## 73. The store refuses a newer version and commits durably; the gateway samples its own health; the QUIC refusal is watched; an encrypted copy leaves the box

*2026-11-08, from research pass 5 (internal note sections 3.4, 5.2 and 7 item 10; external note B6 and C), the reliability floor, and from the coordinator's reading of the reference gateway that morning (the gateway's own lookups at a reboot). Nothing here refuses the TV anything or changes `tvgw-apply`'s firewall; the one new table, behind `VPN_DNS_SEAL`, which is off, refuses only the gateway's own plain lookups. The store's schema for it is 14.*

**Context.** Several gaps could cost the record or the evening without a line saying so. `db.connect()` wrote `SCHEMA_VERSION` into `meta` on every writer's open, so an older tvgw installed over a newer one, which is how a bad release is rolled back today ([decision 53](#53-releases-carry-a-hash-checked-bootstrap-for-two-architectures-with-provenance-under-immutable-releases-updates-are-checked-weekly-installed-only-by-the-user-judged-by-verify-and-rolled-back-a-new-settings-key-never-changes-the-meaning-of-an-old-keys-value)), relabelled the store with its own lower number and left the newer tables and rows behind with nobody responsible for them. Nothing sampled the gateway's temperature, its throttling, its memory, the card's writes or the kernel's disk errors and USB resets, on an overclocked Pi that had one watchdog reset (2026-10-31) beside a USB disk failing its SMART checks since 2026-10-25. The store ran `synchronous=FULL` with its rollback journal ([decision 10](#10-a-rollback-journal-not-wal)), which makes a commit atomic but not durable: the commit is the unlink of the journal, FULL does not sync the directory after it, and a cut before the filesystem journals that unlink brings the journal back, so the next open rolls the committed transaction back. Nothing had ever cut the power under the store, the settings file or the machine's rules file, and the rules file and the live reading's give-up counts were renamed into place without an fsync.

**Decision.**

- **The version only goes up.** A writer reads `meta.schema` before it writes anything, the journal mode included, and raises `db.StoreTooNew`, a subclass of `sqlite3.Error`, having written nothing, when the version is higher than `SCHEMA_VERSION` or is not a whole number. Every command that writes the store says so in one line and exits 1 (`cli.main()`); `tvgw verify --record` prints the line as the run it could not record; the analyst's two writer connections now go through `db.connect()` too. A reader still opens such a store: reading changes nothing and the schema only adds. The version row is written only when it rises, so a writer's open of an up-to-date store writes nothing, where every open used to replace the row, a journal and three fsyncs on the card each time. `tvgw verify` gains `store_version`, which FAILs at once, not held at WARN as the new checks are, when the store is newer than the code, naming both versions and the two ways out: a rollback deploy ends with a plain `tvgw verify`, which reads the store and would otherwise pass while every writer refused it.
- **`synchronous=EXTRA`.** NORMAL is out with a rollback journal: SQLite documents a window for corruption on power loss there, and only WAL makes it safe. WAL stays out for decision 10's reason, unprivileged readers. Between FULL and EXTRA the difference is one fsync of the directory per commit, after the journal's unlink, and it is what makes an acknowledged commit survive a cut; every writer now opens with EXTRA (`db.SYNCHRONOUS`). Its cost is measured by `tools/sync_bench.py`, which commits one small row per transaction in each mode and, on Linux, reads the device's own counters of sectors written and cache flushes per commit. On the development machine (macOS, APFS, whose fsync does not flush the drive's cache, 2026-11-08, 300 commits a mode) FULL and EXTRA both took a median of about 0.3 ms a commit, which says only that the order costs nothing there; the card's number is the gateway's to give, by the bench run on a directory of the card. The store commits a few dozen times an hour (an ingest per batch and per pcap, a verify run, an hourly check), so EXTRA adds a few dozen directory fsyncs an hour; the up-to-date open above takes away the commit every writer's open used to make, about ten an hour with two or three fsyncs each, so the total stays about where it was.
- **A file replaced whole, by one order.** `durable.replace()` writes a new file beside the old one, fsyncs it, sets its mode and owner, renames it over the old name and fsyncs the directory. The machine's rules file (`autoblock`), its restore after a failed site-rule change (`localrules.write_auto`) and the live reading's give-up counts (`tvlive.write_progress`, whose loss would buy the TV fresh knocks on its pairing) go through it now; the settings file (`discover.write_conf`), the site rules and AdGuard Home's configuration already kept that order.
- **The power cut, tested.** LazyFS, which the research proposed, is a research prototype tested on Ubuntu 20.04 that CI would have to build from a third party's source, and its README does not say whether a cut loses a rename or an unlink, which is exactly the loss in question; it was not used. The namespace tier cuts at the block device instead, with the kernel's own device-mapper target and no new code from outside: ext4 on a loop device behind device-mapper, and the cut is dm-flakey's `drop_writes`, the way the kernel's filesystem tests cut power (`tests/virtual/test_virtual_powercut.py`). Every writer is the gateway's code in its own process. With EXTRA the store keeps every acknowledged commit, whole, also when cut in the middle of a transaction; with FULL, the control, the last acknowledged commit rolls back. The settings file, the rules file and the give-up counts come back as the acknowledged version; a rename without an fsync, the second control, loses it. The unit tier kills each writer with SIGKILL at fixed random instants (`tests/unit/test_killed_writers.py`), which proves the order and the transactions but not a cut, since a kill keeps the page cache; a file written in place is its control. Neither models a card that acknowledges a flush it did not do: no software order survives that, and only a better card or a battery-backed one does. The gate's reboot list that the research named does not exist yet; when it is built it is written with `durable.replace()`, and its writer joins the cut's cases.
- **The gateway's own health, sampled** (`src/tvgw/health.py`, schema 14). `tvgw verify --record`'s sample gains nine columns: the hottest thermal zone, the Raspberry Pi firmware's throttle flags (`vcgencmd get_throttled`), `MemAvailable`'s share and the kernel's cumulative memory-pressure stall, the disk under `STATE_DIR` and its sectors written since boot, and the kernel's lines since the previous sample, across boots, that report a disk error (on any disk, and on the record's) and a USB reset (`journalctl _TRANSPORT=kernel`; `-k` would read this boot alone and miss the minutes before a crash). Each source is optional, as the sampler's others are, and a source that cannot say is NULL, never zero. `health.findings()` reads a window against the one as long before it and names only what needs the owner: the firmware slowing the gateway (a condition holding at a sample, or newly happened since boot, so an old event does not come back every day), any disk error on the record's disk, and disk errors elsewhere or USB resets that more than doubled against the window before (`health.rising()`), so the reference's USB disk, which fails at its own pace, is counted and shown every day and raised only when it starts or gets markedly worse. `tvgw verify` gains `gateway_health`, a WARN at most and never promoted, over the last 24 hours; the brief gains a `Gateway health` line with the numbers and the red line `gateway hardware:` in the doctor's tier, its one plain line; the analyst's pack carries it as a health line that buys no request. Temperature, memory and the card's writes are shown and are never findings: no threshold for them was measured on this hardware, and a number the owner learns to ignore is worse than none.
- **The QUIC refusal, watched** (research pass 5, external note B6). UDP 443 stays refused, so apps fall back to TCP where the server name shows; an app that never falls back would fail silently one evening, and Media over QUIC, piloted in 2026, may not fall back. The note proposed a sampled log rule before the reject; none is needed, because the capture sees the TV's QUIC Initial before the firewall refuses it and the extractor already stores its hello (`tls.transport` `quic`). `queries.quic_fallback()` judges each attempt (one address and source port) by a TCP connection to TCP 443 at the same address, or a TCP hello naming the same registrable domain, within `FALLBACK_WINDOW`, 10 seconds, or by a TCP connection of the same domain or address already open across the attempt (an app that keeps its connection needs no fallback; the review of 2026-11-08 found it read as a failure), and names the domains with `NEVER_MIN`, 3, attempts and none followed. The brief carries one engineering line; `tvgw verify`'s `quic_fallback` warns over the last week, the newest 2 hours left out, saying how many names and never which, since its detail reaches the public hardware log. Nothing changes in the firewall. Old-style Google QUIC, which the extractor does not read, stays outside the watch (owed test T5).
