- **An encrypted copy leaves the box, and a drill proves a copy restores.** The daily copies sit on the card that holds the store; October's research adopted an encrypted pull to the owner's computer, and none existed (`make backup` pulled plain copies by rsync). `tvgw backup --export` hands the newest checked copy to `age`, encrypted to `BACKUP_RECIPIENT`, the owner's age recipient (checked by the export alone, never by `config.load`, so a mistyped optional key stops nothing else), on standard output; `make backup` runs it over ssh and names the file only once the command succeeded, so only ciphertext crosses and lands, and the key that opens it never touches the gateway. `age` because it is one small program in Debian 12 and 13 and in Homebrew, encrypts to a public key, so the gateway holds no secret and needs no passphrase, and authenticates what it encrypts, which `openssl enc` does not; no new Python dependency. The old plain pull stays as `make backup-plain`. `tvgw restore --check FILE [--identity KEY]` restores a copy, plain or encrypted, into a scratch directory: `PRAGMA integrity_check`, the version rule above, an open as this tvgw's writer, which migrates it, the check again, and its rows counted; the scratch is removed whatever happens, and the file is only read. It reads no settings, so it runs on the owner's computer, where the key is; the owner step is on [maintenance](use/maintenance.md#keep-a-copy-on-another-computer). The drill is the owner's to run and record; nothing on the gateway can see it.
- **Ready for the next kernel and the next AdGuard.** Linux 6.17 made legacy iptables a kernel build option, which a distribution's kernel may leave out (external note C); tvgw is nftables alone, but a VPN client's kill switch on the same box may not be. `tvgw verify` gains `iptables_legacy`, a WARN at most: a legacy table in `/proc/net/ip_tables_names` or its two siblings, or an `iptables` that is the legacy variant, says to find its owner before a kernel upgrade. AdGuard Home's 1.0 betas are tracked against the pin, never installed: the AdGuard page lists the three things to check before a stable 1.0 is pinned, the schema, the TCP prefix fix's effect on the in-path filler's `unparsed_tcp`, and the DDR fix's on the owed test T8.
- **The gateway's own lookups while the tunnel is down, held for a bounded time, behind `VPN_DNS_SEAL`.** Measured on the reference gateway by the coordinator on 2026-11-08: the red line `the Pi's own lookups left outside the VPN: DNS left by the uplink 32 time(s)` came from a planned reboot at 2026-11-07 19:40, when the VPN client stopped during the shutdown and the gateway's own port-53 lookups left by the uplink until it halted, and possibly at boot before the tunnel came up; every reboot repeats it. With `VPN_DNS_SEAL=on` (and `VPN_IF` not `none`) a table of its own, `inet tvgw_seal`, which neither `tvgw-apply` nor `tvgw.service`'s stop removes, refuses the gateway's own new connections to port 53 leaving by `WAN_IF` while its set holds port 53, and each element carries a timeout: what is left of `HOLD_SECONDS`, 300, for the tunnel-down period, all its holds together, counted on the boot clock from its first hold (`/run/tvgw/seal-since`), so uplink flaps or VPN restarts while the tunnel stays down cannot keep it going; once spent, a hold says so in the journal and holds nothing, and the tunnel's `up` ends the period. The dispatcher puts the port in on `WAN_IF`'s `up`, before the tunnel can be up, and takes it out on the tunnel's `up`; `tvgw-vpnseal.service`, ordered after `piavpn.service` and part of it, puts it in when it stops, which systemd does before it stops the client: at shutdown and at every stop or restart of the client. Its start loads the table and takes the port out when the tunnel is up, so a restart never leaves a hold behind a working tunnel; with the setting off its `ExecCondition` leaves it inactive, so no Python runs before a VPN client's stop. The refusal is a reject, so the C library's resolver, on a connected socket, moves on at once instead of waiting.
  - **What it never holds, by construction.** The TV's lookups: they reach AdGuard Home on the gateway, which asks its upstreams by DoH on port 443, and the table names no port but 53, so a VPN that never comes up costs the TV nothing, and with it AdGuard's cache, the list updates and the TV's store downloads. The coordinator's brief offered refusing the gateway's DoH on the uplink as well; that would take the TV's lookups away while the tunnel is down, from an empty cache after a boot, which the hard rule of this change forbids, so the DoH leak stays counted and not refused. Some users are never held: nginx's (`www-data`), which resolves the vendor's file-service names for the URL filter, so the TV's app store keeps working through an outage; and the clock's, `systemd-timesync`, or chrony's `_chrony` or `chrony`, so the gateway's clock, on which every certificate check of the TV's DoH depends, does not wait on a VPN. Under systemd-resolved every lookup is resolved's own, which cannot be exempt without exempting everything, so there the clock's lookups are held with the rest, for 5 minutes at most. `config.load` refuses `VPN_DNS_SEAL=on` with any `DNS_UPSTREAMS` item named by host: AdGuard looks such a name up in plain DNS on port 53 as root (its bootstrap), a hold would refuse it, and the TV's new lookups would wait (the review of 2026-11-08). AdGuard's own downloads of its lists, where they resolve the list's host in plain DNS, wait for a hold to end; the lists it has stay in force. Each is a numeric uid in the rule, looked up when it is written, since nft refuses a whole table over a user name it cannot resolve.
  - **It fails open.** A hold ends by the kernel's own clock: a tunnel not back within 5 minutes, or a VPN client that never starts, gets today's behaviour, the gateway's lookups leaving by the uplink and counted. Nothing waits on tvgw for it. A VPN client that needs a plain lookup to connect waits up to 5 minutes for it after a boot or its own restart.
  - **What leaks with it on.** While the tunnel is down: the TV's lookups as DoH by address, encrypted, so the network sees connections to the DoH resolver and not the names (the DoH leak counter); nginx's lookups of the vendor's file-service names and the clock's of its time servers, in plain DNS; and every other plain lookup of the gateway's once a hold has ended. Only `piavpn.service` is ordered; another client's stop is not held, and its tunnel's going is seen only at the uplink's next start. Plain lookups that a VPN client sends to a resolver on the home network while its tunnel is up leave by the uplink at any time; while held they are refused, which is why the dispatcher releases when the tunnel comes up.
  - **Why behind a setting.** It refuses traffic of the gateway's own that worked before, and whether a few minutes of refused lookups at every boot and every VPN restart are worth the leak they close is the owner's call. `off`, the default, is the gateway as it was.

**Consequences.**

- After a rollback over a newer store, every writer stops with the same line until the newer tvgw is back or a copy from before it is put in place; the brief and the other readers go on reading.
- One more fsync a commit, the directory's, on the card, against the commit an up-to-date open no longer makes. Whether a commit is durable now rests on the card honouring its flushes.
- The sampler runs two more read-only commands every 10 minutes, `vcgencmd get_throttled` where it exists (only `throttled=0x...` is read, never an error's text) and `journalctl _TRANSPORT=kernel` over the minutes since the last sample, and reads a handful of files in `/proc` and `/sys`; nine columns more per row, about 144 rows a day under `DB_DAYS`. A disk that logs thousands of errors makes the journal read longer, bounded by its 10-second timeout, past which the counts are NULL.
- `make backup` now needs `BACKUP_RECIPIENT` and `age` on the gateway, and pulls one copy, the newest, where it pulled every copy; an owner who wants the old behaviour runs `make backup-plain`. A pulled copy opens only with the owner's key: a lost key is a lost copy.
- An address a CDN shares counts another app's TCP connection to it as a fallback, which can hide a failure.
- The QUIC watch is only as good as the capture: an hour the capture missed has no attempts, and a fallback whose TCP connection has neither a stored hello nor a stored flow in the window reads as none. A name with fewer than three attempts is never said to fail.
- With `VPN_DNS_SEAL=on`: up to 5 minutes, at each boot and each stop or restart of the VPN client, in which the gateway's own plain lookups by the uplink fail (apt, the update check, the DoH list's download, the CI runner), nginx and the clock excepted; the brief's leak red line should stop firing at reboots. One more unit, `tvgw-vpnseal.service`, which with the setting off removes a table that is not there. Its namespace test (`tests/virtual/test_virtual_seal.py`) proves the refusal, the TV's lookups and web traffic passing, the tunnel's path passing, and the three ways a hold ends; that the stop runs before PIA's at a real shutdown only the gateway can show.
- The power-cut test runs only in CI's namespace tier, as root, with `dmsetup`, `e2fsprogs` and the `dm_flakey` module, which the workflow installs and loads; its numbers are a virtual disk's.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| Whether the gateway's own plain lookups are held off the uplink, for up to 5 minutes each time, while the VPN's tunnel is down | `VPN_DNS_SEAL` | `off`: the lookups leave by the uplink and are counted, as before |
| The age key a copy that leaves the gateway is encrypted to; the owner keeps the key that opens it | `BACKUP_RECIPIENT` | empty: no export; `make backup-plain` pulls plain copies as before |

## 75. The inventions not built are measured first: five report-only experiments, an evidence file chained by hash and kept local, request drafts tvgw never sends, and fail-closed drills in CI

*2026-11-08, from research pass 5 (its package of pass 4's remaining inventions; internal note sections 3.4, 3.5, 3.7 and 4.8) and research pass 4's invention table, whose "cheapest experiment" column each item below builds. Built, report-only: nothing refuses, holds or sends anything, and no setting is added. No table is added.*

**Context.** Research pass 4's invention table listed candidates that no product or paper was found to do in the same form, each with its closest prior art, its main risk and the cheapest experiment that would show whether it is worth building. Pass 5 builds the walls that have evidence; eight candidates had none yet. A rule that holds every new name, opens a platform host only in some states, or classifies a name by its neighbours can break an evening, and [the two-week counting rule](#20-the-gateway-blocks-on-its-own-under-three-watches) decides when a wall may refuse. Each of these starts one step earlier: does the store show the rule would have anything to do, and at what cost? Three of the candidates need no rule at all (an evidence file, request drafts, drills), but each carries its own risk: a file that describes the household, a letter that identifies it to a company, and a drill that does not recover on a live gateway.

**Decision.**

- **One command reads the store for five experiments, and changes nothing.** `tvgw experiment NAME` (`src/tvgw/experiments.py`) opens the store `mode=ro` like every read command. Its only other work is the ACR meter's positive control, built in a store held in memory and thrown away. The five are:
  - **probation**, the evidence for new-name probation: registrable domains first seen (`hosts.first_seen`, of any name under the domain) after the record's first `WARMUP_DAYS = 14` calendar days, by ISO week, each with its class (`appfront.classify`), the company behind it, whether the filter refused every lookup, some or none, and the connections a hello named. A domain the filter answered at least once is one a hold-until-judged rule would have held: the weekly count of those is that rule's cost. A must-allow host among them is counted apart, since such a rule would have to exempt it, and so is a domain first reached with no lookup in the store, since a rule at the resolver cannot hold it. Research pass 5's internal note (3.5) expects the remaining case to be the `other` class, because the lists already refuse tracking names; the count says how often it appears.
  - **platform**, the evidence for just-in-time platform access: lookups of, and named connections to, each must-allow host and each clock host, by the TV's state at that moment (an app in front, the home screen, an input, a cast receiver, the screen saver, off, unknown) from `appfront.timeline()` over the live record and the hourly readings, with the hours in each state. A name both lists cover is counted once, under its must-allow host. A state with no contacts is one in which the host was not contacted in those days (few hours in that state, an answer the TV kept, or a connection opened in another state can explain it); nothing is scheduled.
  - **fanout**, fan-out association for unclassified names: for each registrable domain the profile has no category for and the filter answered, its episodes (lookups within `FANOUT_EPISODE_GAP = 10` seconds are one), once it has `FANOUT_MIN_EPISODES = 3` on `FANOUT_MIN_DAYS = 2` days, and the classified names and refused lookups within `FANOUT_WINDOW = 2.0` seconds before or after each. A neighbour's base rate is the share of the TV's other lookups that have one of the neighbour's lookups within the same window (`base_rate()`), and `lift` is the share of the unclassified name's episodes the neighbour comes next to, over that rate. The base is anchored on lookups, not on time, because the TV looks names up in bursts and a burst puts every name near every other: a base taken over time read names that only share busy moments at a lift of 1.08 to 1.49 in a review's simulation, and the test of this one (bursts with the two names placed independently) reads between 0.6 and 1.6. Only a neighbour at `FANOUT_MIN_LIFT = 2.0` or more is shown or counts. The ten most associated are ranked by the episodes beside such a neighbour that was refused or is filed under tracking (the TV maker's own names are shown, not counted), and every report ends with the sentence that this is association, not proof: a player's own server comes shortly before its ad calls every time.
  - **acr**, the "ACR uploads per viewing hour" meter: what the TV sent names in the profile's ACR category beyond what came back on the same connections (`budget.net_up()`, [design decision 66](#66-an-upload-budget-with-a-ceiling-set-once-count-only-and-the-names-of-a-tv-that-goes-online-while-off)), by the state the TV was in when each connection opened. The rate is the net of the connections opened while the TV was on (the screen saver included) over the hours it was on, the same states on both sides; what opened while it was off or in a gap is printed apart, since with hourly readings most of a day is a gap. The uploads the ACR test flags (`queries.acr_uploader()`, [design decision 45](#45-the-acr-red-line-is-an-upload-test-and-a-port-wall)) are counted with their payload and carry no rate, since the test buckets by hour and day, not by state. The lookups of ACR names, answered and refused, are apart (a lookup is an attempt, not an upload), and so are the TV's Live Plus reading and consent flags. Above the reading stands the record it was taken over (lookups, connections, those a hello named, capture files read): a zero over a window with no connections says nothing, and the report says so. With the agreements declined the expected reading is zero, so beside it stands a positive control: one hour of 4,125,000 bytes of frames both ways at 7 up to 1 down, every frame full-size, on 60 connections with an input in front, the corner `ACR_HOUR_NET` is estimated at (`CONTROL_FRAMES`, `CONTROL_RATIO`), run through the same arithmetic. On `lg-webos` it reads 2,982,780 bytes per hour on, a little above `ACR_HOUR_NET` because the payload bound takes 40 header bytes off each packet where a full frame with TCP timestamps carries 52, and the ACR test flags it once; the tests hold it equal to `tests/unit/acr_synth.py`'s corner hour through the same meter. It is built from published figures, not taken through the capture and the ingest, so it shows only that the arithmetic sees such an hour; the record line shows how much the capture gave it to see. A profile with no ACR category reads 0 named and still 1 flagged: only the shape test can see it there. The public LG trace stays out of the repository, as decision 45 kept it.
  - **firmware**, the before-and-after report: for each software change the hourly reading recorded (`queries.firmware_changes()`), the `FIRMWARE_DAYS = 14` days before against the days after, from `UPDATE_LEAD = 3600` seconds before the reading that found it (the reading sees an update that happened since the one before it): new and vanished registrable domains, (protocol, port) pairs to public servers, TLS stacks (JA4 of every hello, named or not) and record types, and lookups a day. The A/A floor is the latest two adjacent 14-day windows with no change inside them that the record covers. A count is marked above the floor when it is more than `FLOOR_TIMES = 2` times the floor and at least its feature's `FLOOR_MARGIN` over it: 3 for domains, since a quiet fortnight brings new delivery networks of its own, and 1 for ports, stacks and record types, which a quiet fortnight rarely brings (the reference TV asked for no record type but A and AAAA over its whole store to 2026-11-05, as the changelog's closures of that day record). A window with lookups on fewer than `FIRMWARE_MIN_DAYS = 10` of its days is marked thin. The reference TV's firmware predates the gateway, so today only the A/A half has data.
- **The evidence file is local, append-only, and chained.** `tvgw evidence` (`src/tvgw/evidence.py`) appends to `/var/lib/tvgw/evidence/evidence.jsonl` (root's, `600` in a `700` directory) one line per settled local day (its end `SETTLE`, 3 hours, ago) of the last `--days` (30) the file does not have, never before the record starts. A line is never written again, so a day is written only when the record covers it (`uncovered()`): the hourly ingest ran during the day, ran again after it settled, and no run from the day's start to that one warned that connections or lookups were lost (`LOSS_PREFIXES`: a log rotated past, malformed lines, conntrack's lost events, the resolver's log missing or failing). Without that, a failed ingest (the reference gateway's USB disk is failing its checks) would write a day empty or partial for good, and a clock stepped forward would write empty days the ingest never ran in and then pass over the real ones. A day that fails is named in the run's output with the reason and left out: a gap in the file, which the header says is a gap in the record. A day line holds the TV's model and firmware; its Live Plus reading and consent flags at the day's end, and the flags accepted that day (`queries.consent_flips()`); per tracking domain (`plain.is_tracking()`), the lookups refused and answered, the connections a hello named and their net upload; the ACR test's findings; the day's own destination-hours over the upload budget, with no hour, at the ceiling in force that day (the floor for a day before the fixed ceiling was set; a connection that completed an earlier day's hour belongs to that day); and how much record the day has (lookups, connections, the share of TLS connections named, TV readings, ingest runs). Names are registrable domains; there is no address of the home, no time of day and no record of which app was in front, though a destination over the budget or flagged by the ACR test can be an app's own server, and the day's counts show how much the TV was used. The first line is a header that says, for a reader who has never seen the gateway, what was observed (lookups, named connections, the TV's readings) and what was estimated (bytes beyond what came back), that a refused lookup is an attempt the gateway stopped and so a zero upload to a tracker the gateway refuses shows nothing about the TV, and what the chain can and cannot show. Each line is written in one canonical form (`canonical()`: sorted keys, no spaces, ASCII) and holds the SHA-256 of the line above in `prev` (the header's is 64 zeros). `tvgw evidence --verify` names the first line changed, put in or reordered without the chain being computed again, a cut-off last line, a byte that is not ASCII, or a version it does not know. The chain has no key: anyone who can write the file can compute it again, and a file with its last lines cut off still checks. It holds against tampering only up to a head hash the owner writes down off the gateway: every run prints the last line's SHA-256, and `--verify --head HASH` checks that a line still has it. A run refuses a file whose chain is broken, or that holds a byte that is not ASCII, and appends nothing, under an exclusive lock, with each line flushed to disk.
- **No anchoring, by design.** A timestamp from a public service would prove the file existed at a time, and would also tell that service, and anyone watching, that this home runs the gateway at that moment ([design decision 63](#63-behavior-changing-deploys-to-the-reference-wait-for-a-quiet-window-decided-on-the-gateway-nothing-the-gateway-prints-where-others-can-read-it-carries-a-home-identifier-acceptance-that-needs-systemd-runs-in-a-container-tier)). What the chain shows, and when the lines were written, rests on the owner's own record of a head hash. Research pass 4 judged the risk the same way: the anchoring step stays manual and optional.
- **Request drafts say first what identifies the household, and are never sent.** `tvgw requests` (`src/tvgw/drafts.py`) lists, over `--days` (30), the companies the TV reached (a connection a hello named that carried data both ways, or a plain-HTTP request that got a success or redirect answer, so a connection the firewall refused, a QUIC opening `BLOCK_QUIC` rejected or a request the URL filter refused never counts), the ones it only attempted (every lookup refused and nothing reached: the TV sent them nothing through the gateway, so no draft is written), and the ones looked up and answered with nothing reached, which the record cannot place. The company is the shipped snapshot's (`companies.company()`); a name it does not know is counted under its registrable domain, never guessed. `--draft` writes one plain-text draft per company reached into `/var/lib/tvgw/requests/<day>/` (or a new or empty directory `--out` names; never over a file), each `600` in a `700` directory, with a `README.txt`. Each draft opens with what sending it would tell the company (who the owner is, that this household owns this TV, and, in one paragraph the owner may delete, that a gateway watched the TV), says that tvgw never sends a draft and does not know the company's address, and is a template, not legal advice. Every identifier (name, email, account, the TV's model and serial number) is a placeholder; tvgw fills in none, not even the model it reads. The letter lists the company's own registrable domains with the days each was reached, and the ones of its the gateway refused.
- **`tvgw forget` deletes both.** `evidence` and `requests` are forgotten state entries (`forget.STATE_ENTRIES`); a copy made by hand, or drafts written with `--out` outside the state directory, are listed among what tvgw cannot reach.
- **The fail-closed drills run in CI only.** `tests/virtual/test_virtual_drills.py` kills each part that runs beside the firewall with SIGKILL on the namespace gateway (the in-path filler, the passive feeder, the live reading, the hourly job with `tvgw ingest` at work on a large flow log, and the stand-in for AdGuard Home) and probes the TV's internet before, during and after: a lookup at the gateway, a lookup aimed at a public resolver, and a connection by address. The table of what the TV keeps when each part fails is [when a part fails](how-it-works/architecture.md#when-a-part-fails), and a unit test holds every row to a drill, every drill to a row, and the restart settings the table quotes to the unit files. No drill runs on the live gateway: research pass 4's main risk for this candidate was a drill that does not recover there, an outage nobody is told about.

**Consequences.**

- Nothing changes for any gateway that does not run the commands: no unit, timer, table, setting or brief line is added, and the brief and its JSON are byte for byte what they were. The experiments, `evidence --verify` and `requests` without `--draft` read the store as any member of `adm` may; `evidence` and `requests --draft` write under the state directory, as root.
- The experiments decide nothing. Each is the measurement a later decision would rest on: a probation rule, a schedule for a platform host, the analyst reading an association, a firmware tripwire. None is wired into a rule here.
- The evidence file grows by a few kilobytes a day and is never pruned, since a line taken out breaks every line after it: it is kept until `tvgw forget` or the owner removes it, and it outlives `DB_DAYS` for the days it already holds. It holds no time of day, but it does say, day by day, which tracking companies the TV tried to reach. A day the record does not cover is a gap in the file for good: the file says less rather than something wrong.
- The meter's zero says that the meter saw no upload to a name filed under screen recognition and nothing shaped like one. It cannot see an upload inside a connection that returns as much, or one moved to a name the profile does not file under screen recognition and shaped unlike the test; and with the gateway refusing the ACR names, it cannot show what the TV would have sent without it.
- A draft lists the domains of one company only. Sent, it identifies the household to that company; tvgw cannot know what the company does with it.
- The drills prove each row on a kernel in CI, not on the reference gateway; the row for AdGuard Home's restart rests on its unit's settings, not on a drill of systemd.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| Whether and how often to add to the evidence file (no timer runs it) | no setting (`sudo tvgw evidence`) | by hand |
| Whether to send any draft, and with which identifiers | no setting | never sent by tvgw |
| What each experiment's numbers decide: a probation rule, a platform schedule, a firmware tripwire | no setting | nothing; each would be a decision of its own |

## 76. The brief on your phone: a read-only page of its own on the home network, behind HTTPS and a password, closed to the TV and off by default

*2026-11-08, from the competitive-gaps report of that day (its brief G1), under the owner's direction of that day: "If there are gaps where other apps like Firewalla, Pi-Hole, and others are way ahead, I want to close them." Built: `tvgw web`, its unit and its check, off unless `WEB=on`. Pending: the owner's answer to D29 of the 2026-11-03 improvement design (a page readable without a terminal), so every gateway keeps `WEB=off` until then. No store schema change: the sessions live in memory.*

**Context.** Every product tvgw is compared with has a screen on the phone: Firewalla's and UniFi's apps, and the web pages of Pi-hole, AdGuard Home and OpenWrt. Pi-hole v6 serves its page from its own web server with HTTPS, a password, TOTP and app passwords (https://pi-hole.net/blog/2025/02/18/introducing-pi-hole-v6/, https://docs.pi-hole.net/api/auth/); AdGuard Home's 1.0 beta adds a phone layout (https://adguard.com/en/blog/adguard-home-beta-version-1-0.html); both read by the report on 2026-11-08. tvgw needed an SSH login and a terminal every day, so a typical owner never read the brief, which is where every other finding lands. The 2026-11-03 design's W16 recommended D29 option (a): a static page served by nginx on port 8080 behind a secret link setup prints once, plain HTTP, written to `/run/tvgw/www` by `tvgw-verify.service`.

**Decision.**

- **A process of its own, never nginx.** `tvgw web serve`, run by `tvgw-web.service` as the system user `tvgw-web` (made by `install.sh`, in `adm` through `SupplementaryGroups=` to read the settings and the store as `tvgw brief` does), on the standard library's `ThreadingHTTPServer` with `ssl`. It refuses to serve as root. nginx faces the TV (it is the URL filter, [design decision 8](#8-a-url-filter-for-lgs-file-service-instead-of-a-dns-block)), so a page in its process would put a parser the TV reaches beside one that holds the household's record.
- **Never on the TV's side, and only for the home.** It binds `WAN_IF`'s IPv4 address, read at start by the `SIOCGIFADDR` ioctl (no subprocess, no netlink socket), or with `WEB_BIND=loopback` 127.0.0.1 alone, and refuses (`web.check_bind`, exit 2, which its unit never retries) a wildcard, `GW_IP`, any address in `TV_NET`, and any address outside 10/8, 172.16/12 and 192.168/16, so a gateway whose uplink holds a public or a carrier's shared address serves nothing. Three walls keep the TV out: `inet tvgw`'s input chain (`TV talks to the gateway address only`), the guard's (`guard: the gateway address only (weak host)`, [design decision 42](#42-the-pi-is-closed-to-the-tv-whatever-nftables-holds)), and the unit's own cgroup filter, `IPAddressDeny=any` with `IPAddressAllow=` loopback and the three private ranges less `TV_NET` (`tvgw web dropin`, written by `install.sh` into `tvgw-web.service.d/not-from-tv.conf`). systemd lets an allowed address through whatever the deny list says, so `TV_NET` is cut out of the ranges rather than denied beside them. The same drop-in sets `RestrictNetworkInterfaces=lo <WAN_IF>` (loopback alone with `WEB_BIND=loopback`), so `TV_IF` is never one of its interfaces, and `SocketBindAllow=ipv4:tcp:<WEB_PORT>` beside the unit's `SocketBindDeny=any`. The unit makes the logs, the journal, the captures, the copies, the reports, the pairing key and the two other services' secrets inaccessible (`InaccessiblePaths=`). The page's user is in `adm`, so it reads the whole store, as every member of `adm` does: a compromise of its process reads the record, which is why it holds no capability, writes nothing, and reaches nothing but the home. The NetworkManager dispatcher restarts the page, without waiting, when the uplink's address is new or changed.
- **HTTPS with the owner's own certificate.** `sudo tvgw web cert` makes a self-signed P-256 certificate with `openssl` for 730 days, naming the gateway's host name with and without `.local`, `localhost`, 127.0.0.1 and the uplink's address, CA:FALSE, digital signature and server authentication only; the key is `0640 root:tvgw-web`, and the command prints the SHA-256 fingerprint for the owner to compare on the phone's one warning. That is trust on first use: the comparison is with what the command printed on the gateway, never with anything the page shows, since whoever served a page could show any fingerprint (the About page says so, and shows none). The `.local` name is the one to open, since a certificate naming the uplink's address warns again after the router hands out a new one. TLS 1.2 or newer, ECDHE with AES-GCM or ChaCha20 for 1.2, no compression, no renegotiation. No HSTS: a new certificate would lock the phone out.
- **A password, kept as a hash.** `sudo tvgw web password` asks twice for at least 12 characters and stores scrypt (N = 2^15, r = 8, p = 1, a random 16-byte salt, NFC first) in `/etc/tvgw/web-password`, `0640 root:tvgw-web`; a hash file asking for more than 2^20, r 16 or p 4 is refused, never computed. The comparison is constant-time and one check runs at a time. A session is a cookie of 32 random bytes (`__Host-tvgw-session`, `Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict; Path=/`), kept in memory by its hash: 30 days at most, 7 days unused, 20 at once (the one unused longest goes), and every session ends when the password changes. The limit on failed sign-ins (`webauth.Limiter`, after the 2026-11-08 security review): per client address, five free tries, then a wait of 1 minute that doubles after each further failure up to 1 hour, cleared by a success or a day without a failure; for a device the page does not know, one password check every 3 seconds among all such devices and at most 100 failures a day, after which they are refused until the oldest ages out. A signed known-device cookie (`__Host-tvgw-device`: a random id and its HMAC under a key derived from the stored hash, 400 days) spares the owner's devices those two limits; it survives a restart of the page, ends with a new password, and signs nobody in. Each check is reserved under the limiter's lock before the slow hash and counted as a failure at once, so guesses sent together wait their turn (sixteen at once used to be checked sixteen times); a refused attempt is never hashed or counted. The sign-in form carries an HMAC of a cookie set with it, under a key the process alone holds; sign-out is a POST with the session's own token; a POST whose `Origin` names another site, or whose `Sec-Fetch-Site` is `cross-site`, is refused.
- **Five read-only pages, no JavaScript.** Today (`/`: `tvgw status`'s three lines, then yesterday's plain brief with its Action first and large), a day (`/day/YYYY-MM-DD`: its plain brief, the days either side, a 14-day table of hours on, tracker lookups refused and new services from the plain brief's own functions, the day's new names, and the engineering brief at `/full`), a name (`/name/<host>`: kind, company, first and last seen, blocked or allowed and by which kind of rule from the store's record of its newest answer, lookups per day for 14 days, and the command to copy), your rules (`/rules`: `tvgw rules --local`) and about. One stylesheet, a viewport tag, light and dark by `prefers-color-scheme`.
- **Read-only by construction.** The store is opened `mode=ro` with `query_only`; no page writes a file, runs a command or takes SQL. Every value from the record goes through `webpages.esc` (`cli.clean`, then HTML escaping with quotes): a TV app chooses the names it looks up. A name is a link only when it is a plain host name. Every response, errors included, carries `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self'; form-action 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'; base-uri 'none'`, `nosniff`, `no-referrer`, `no-store`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY` and a `Server` header without Python's version. A `Host` that names neither the address it listens on nor the gateway's own names is answered 421 (another site's name pointed at the gateway). It shows what the daily report shows: never the app-in-front sessions ([design decision 67](#67-the-tvs-own-account-of-itself-is-the-ground-sessions-a-receipt-and-a-replay-keyed-to-the-app-in-front-a-live-reading-that-never-knocks-blind-no-rule-yet)).
- **Bounded, and second to the gateway's own work.** A 10-second socket timeout on each read, and a deadline for the whole request: one thread shuts each connection's socket 15 seconds after accept (`REQUEST_DEADLINE`, the TLS handshake included, which runs in the connection's own thread), relaxed to 90 seconds once the request is read so a brief slow to build is not cut off; 32 connections at once and 4 from one address (closed unread beyond), 16 requests at once (503 beyond); the request line and headers together at most 16 KB (431 beyond, having read no more), where the standard library would take 100 lines of 64 KB; GET, HEAD and POST only (405 with `Allow`); POST bodies of 4 KB, urlencoded, with a length; unknown paths 404; HTTP/0.9 refused, since it carries no headers. One brief is built at a time, and a finished day (3 hours after its end, as `tvgw-analyze` waits) is built once and kept, 14 at most, until the store's first ingest changes, which is `tvgw forget`. The unit runs at `Nice=10`, `CPUWeight=20`, `IOWeight=20`, `OOMScoreAdjust=500`, `MemoryMax=256M`, with no capability, nothing writable, `RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET AF_UNIX` and `SystemCallFilter=@system-service`.
- **Nothing from the record in the journal.** The server logs its start (port and which kind of address, no address), its stop, each sign-in's outcome and a page that failed by its exception's type, with the time journald gives the line. The base class's request log is off.
- **Settings, off by default.** `WEB` (`off` or `on`; absent means off; the template sets `off`), `WEB_PORT` (8443, 1024 to 65535) and `WEB_BIND` (`uplink` or `loopback`). `install.sh` makes the user and writes the filter always, and enables the unit only with `WEB=on`, a password and a certificate; with `WEB=off` it disables it. `sudo tvgw web status` says whether it runs, where, the certificate's fingerprint and end, and whether a password is set.
- **A check, WARN first.** `tvgw verify` gains `web` ([design decision 58](#58-the-brief-has-a-plain-form-with-three-tiers-the-login-message-shows-its-status-and-action-and-tvgw-doctor-explains-every-check)'s rule for new checks): with `WEB=off` nothing listens on `WEB_PORT`; with `WEB=on` the unit runs, listens on the uplink's current address or loopback (from `/proc/net/tcp` and `tcp6`) and never on every address, `GW_IP` or `TV_NET` (FAIL level, held at WARN until promoted), the certificate has 30 more days, and a password is set. `cotenants_denied` reads `tvgw-web.service` beside the co-tenants, and now reads `IPAddressAllow` too: an allowed range that reaches into `TV_NET` beyond `GW_IP` opens it again.

**Why not D29's secret link.** Three reasons. nginx faces the TV, so the page must not share its process. On a shared home Wi-Fi anyone with the passphrase can read plain HTTP, the secret link included, and a link is kept in browser history and bookmarks where a password is not; HTTPS and a password keep the page private there. And the record stays inside root and `adm`: the page's user is in `adm`, where nginx's `www-data` is not, so D29's page would have had to be copied out of the record to somewhere nginx could read it.

**Consequences.** On a gateway with `WEB=off`, which is every gateway until its owner answers D29, nothing listens: the deploy makes a user and a drop-in and leaves the unit disabled, and `web` says `off: nothing listens on port 8443`. With it on, a phone on the home network reads the brief in a browser after one certificate warning; a new uplink address warns once more for a page opened by address, not by its `.local` name, until `sudo tvgw web cert` is run again. A restart (a deploy, a new uplink address, a new password) signs everyone out, and a known device signs in again outside the limits for unknown devices. A guesser on the home network gets five tries per address and then waits that double, and every unknown device shares one check every 3 seconds and 100 failures a day, so the owner's known phone is never kept out and a new device is kept out for a day at worst. The page has no IPv6. The namespace test proves the TV cannot connect to the page's port with the firewall loaded and with only the guard; the cgroup filter and the memory a day of use takes are for the gateway to show.

**Left for later**, said on the page's About too: any button that changes something (allow, block, undo, pause, settings), which needs a narrow root helper with a fixed set of verbs and the same lint as `tvgw allow`; a live view of lookups; search over the whole history; charts; two-factor sign-in and more than one account; reaching the page from outside the home, which is the owner's own VPN, since tvgw never opens a router port or uses a relay; a JSON API or a Home Assistant integration; Let's Encrypt certificates, which need public DNS. Never: a notification, push or mail of any kind, and a page for the TV's own browser (the TV is the adversary).

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| Whether the gateway serves the page at all (D29) | `WEB` | `off` |
| Where it listens: the uplink's address, or loopback only (reached through an SSH tunnel) | `WEB_BIND` | `uplink` |
| Its port | `WEB_PORT` | `8443` |

## 77. The gateway installs its own security updates in the quiet window, checks each install and rolls back what it can

*2026-11-08, from the competitive-gaps analysis (its gap G2) and the owner's word of the same day: "I would prefer [updates] installed autonomously. If they are security updates, they need to be installed." It builds item 5 of [decision 53](#53-releases-carry-a-hash-checked-bootstrap-for-two-architectures-with-provenance-under-immutable-releases-updates-are-checked-weekly-installed-only-by-the-user-judged-by-verify-and-rolled-back-a-new-settings-key-never-changes-the-meaning-of-an-old-keys-value) and changes it for new installs (the dated note there). Built the same day. The reference gateway changes nothing until its settings file has the keys.*

**Context.** Firewalla and UniFi update their boxes themselves; Pi-hole shows "update available" in its web footer; AdGuard Home has an updater of its own, which tvgw switches off ([decision 46](#46-the-blocklists-are-watched-and-adguard-changes-only-on-purpose)); OpenWrt 25.12 has `owut`. A typical tvgw owner never runs apt, so a gateway on the home network could go years without its system's security fixes, and nothing told the owner that a newer tvgw or AdGuard Home existed. Decision 53 had planned a weekly check that installs nothing. Installing on its own has a risk particular to this gateway: an upgrade of dnsmasq, nginx or AdGuard Home restarts it, and a restart during a show cuts the TV off. Debian's own unattended-upgrades runs at 06:00 plus up to an hour, a time chosen for nobody in particular, and can restart the computer itself when configured to. Pass 4 found that Firewalla and UniFi document little about rollback.

**Decision.**

1. **What is installed, by `UPDATE_INSTALL`.** `off`, which a settings file without the key means (decision 53, item 6), installs nothing, as before: the system's own unattended-upgrades, if the owner set one up, is left as it is. `security` installs the system's security updates and AdGuard Home's newer patch releases of the minor version it runs. The security updates are those of Debian's and Ubuntu's security archives (and Ubuntu's ESM), and of Raspberry Pi's own archive, which carries a Pi's kernel and firmware fixes and has no security suite of its own. `all`, which the template sets for new installs, also installs the system's other updates from its own release (Debian's point releases and `-updates`, Ubuntu's `-updates`) and, on a gateway installed from the package, tvgw's newer releases. New installs get `all` because a Debian point release is reviewed by its release team before it is published, and because the owner prefers the gateway to keep itself current.
2. **When.** Only inside `DEPLOY_WINDOW` and while the TV is quiet, decided by `quiet.decide` as for a deploy ([decision 63](#63-behavior-changing-deploys-to-the-reference-wait-for-a-quiet-window-decided-on-the-gateway-nothing-the-gateway-prints-where-others-can-read-it-carries-a-home-identifier-acceptance-that-needs-systemd-runs-in-a-container-tier)), with the TV port also watched for 2 minutes. `tvgw-update.timer` starts at the window's start, at a random minute up to 45 minutes before its end (the drop-in `install.sh` writes from `DEPLOY_WINDOW`). A run outside the window, or with the TV in use, installs nothing, and remembers the night as waited when something is known to wait. The owner's `sudo tvgw updates --now` runs the same installs at once and never restarts the computer.
3. **The system's updates go through unattended-upgrades, configured by tvgw.** `install.sh` writes `/etc/apt/apt.conf.d/90tvgw-updates` (`tvgw updates --configure`), and removes it for `off`; the package's removal removes it too. The file holds the origins of the mode, each list cleared first, so no pattern of the system's own widens what is installed. `APT::Periodic::Unattended-Upgrade "0"` keeps apt's own 06:00 job from installing anything, while the lists are still fetched daily. It sets no automatic reboot, no mail and no removal of unused packages, so nothing the gateway needs is removed. `--force-confdef --force-confold` keeps a changed configuration file and never asks about it: unattended-upgrades holds back a package whose configuration file would ask, unless these are set. The run fetches the package lists, runs `unattended-upgrade`, and tells what changed from dpkg's own list before and after. apt has no rollback, so verify runs afterwards and its result is recorded.
4. **AdGuard Home's patch releases.** The gateway reads AdGuard's latest release from GitHub's API. It installs it only when it is a newer patch of the major and minor version that runs, never a pre-release and never a larger change, which is held for the owner and said by `tvgw updates`. Whether a patch is a security release cannot be judged reliably from its notes, so every newer patch of the running minor version is installable. The tarball's SHA-256 must equal its line in the release's `checksums.txt`, and GitHub's digest of the asset where GitHub reports one; `aghinstall.extract` refuses a binary that does not report the release's version. Then comes `adguardcfg.upgrade`, the path of `tvgw adguard upgrade`: the live configuration is checked with the new binary first, both files are backed up, verify runs afterwards, and the old binary and configuration are put back on any new failure. This goes past [decision 52](#52-setup-fetches-adguard-homes-own-release-pinned-and-hash-checked-and-seeds-it-without-the-wizard)'s pin for patch releases only.
5. **tvgw's own releases, on a package install only.** A gateway that runs from a source checkout, such as the reference, is skipped, and `tvgw updates` says how it is updated. On a package install the gateway installs only a release that GitHub marks immutable and that is not a pre-release, whose package's SHA-256 equals its `SHA256SUMS` line and GitHub's digest. The running version's package must be at hand first: kept from the last install in `<STATE_DIR>/packages`, or fetched from its own release and checked the same way; without it nothing is installed. apt installs the package, and its postinst runs `install.sh`, which takes the install lock itself, so this step runs outside the run's lock and last. Verify judges the result once the settling checks have had 5 minutes. A failure, of apt or of verify, reinstalls the kept package with `--allow-downgrades`. The build provenance attestation is not checked on the gateway, which would need `gh` or a Sigstore client; the immutable flag and the two hashes are.
6. **A restart.** When a package asks for one (`/run/reboot-required`), the gateway restarts only at the end of a night's run, only if the TV is still quiet, and only after it has stored what it did; otherwise it waits for the next quiet night. `tvgw-update-boot.service` runs verify after that restart, with the same settling wait, records the result and removes its marker.
7. **What is recorded, and told.** Each install, rollback and restart is a line in the change ledger (`update_installed`, `update_rolled_back` and `update_reboot`, class A; the check after a restart, `update_verified`, class B) and a run in `meta.update_runs`, which keeps the last 60 runs that did or waited for something. The plain brief says a night's run in one `Updates:` line, never the Action; a run of waited nights is said on its first night only. Its New line says "tvgw 0.4.0 is available" on the day the weekly check first saw that version and every 7 days after, and "the gateway's system has security updates waiting since Monday 2 November" once they have waited a week, then weekly. `tvgw updates`, which needs no root, says for tvgw, AdGuard Home and the system what runs, what waits and what was installed when, with the command for each that needs the owner. `tvgw verify` gains `updates`, a WARN at most ([decision 58](#58-the-brief-has-a-plain-form-with-three-tiers-the-login-message-shows-its-status-and-action-and-tvgw-doctor-explains-every-check)): security updates waiting over 14 days, package lists over 30 days old, or the weekly check failing 4 times in a row. With installs on, it also warns when unattended-upgrades is missing, when apt's effective configuration (`apt-config dump`) is not tvgw's, when a run of the last 8 days rolled back or failed, when the check after a restart failed, or when a restart has been needed for over 7 days. The doctor explains the warning and installs nothing. No push alert, mail or notification of any kind.
8. **The weekly check, `UPDATE_CHECK=weekly`** (the template sets it; a file without it means `off`): one GET of `https://api.github.com/repos/<UPDATE_REPO>/releases/latest` a week, with `User-Agent: tvgw/<version>`, `Accept: application/vnd.github+json` and `X-GitHub-Api-Version: 2022-11-28`; no account, token or cookie; a 20-second timeout and a 1 MB cap; redirects to HTTPS only. It stores in `meta.update_check` the latest version, its publication time, the time of the check and the time it first saw that version. A failed check is stored as failed, with its count in a row, and is tried again a week later, never in a loop. It installs nothing.
9. **The system's state, read locally.** `apt-get -s dist-upgrade` with the lists the system already has, without taking apt's lock and without writing apt's cache, counts the packages waiting and those from a security archive or Raspberry Pi's archive; the package lists' age comes from their files. Verify's 10-minute run takes this reading again once a day, or when the lists or dpkg's status changed, and stores it (`meta.system_updates`) with the time security updates were first seen waiting. No request is made for it.

**Consequences.** A new gateway keeps itself current without its owner: its system's fixes arrive the first quiet night after the lists that carry them, AdGuard Home's patch releases the first quiet night after they are published, and on a package install tvgw's releases likewise, each judged by verify, the last two put back on a failure. The price is a nightly window in which the gateway may restart its services and itself. A release that breaks something verify does not test is not rolled back, and a system update never can be. AdGuard Home may run a patch release tvgw was not tested with. The trust root for AdGuard's patches is GitHub and AdGuard's account, since the checksums file comes from the same release and no signature is checked; for tvgw's releases it is GitHub and the account that can push a tag, as for `get.sh` (decision 53). A household whose TV is on every night in the window gets nothing installed: the first waited night is said, the New line names security updates after a week, and verify warns after two. With installs on, the requests added are the system's own mirrors (apt's normal fetch, nightly), GitHub's API once a quiet night for AdGuard's latest release and, on a package install with `all`, for tvgw's, and downloads from GitHub only when something is installed ([what leaves the gateway](how-it-works/privacy-and-retention.md#updates)). The reference gateway changes nothing at the next deploy: its settings file has neither key, so no timer is enabled and apt's configuration stays its own. Its own unattended-upgrades keeps installing security updates at Debian's time, and verify's `updates` check now reads whether they wait.

**Owner decisions, held.**

| Decision | Setting | Until then |
|---|---|---|
| Automatic installs on the reference, in its window (it runs from a checkout, so tvgw's own step is skipped either way) | `UPDATE_INSTALL` | absent, so `off`: the system's own unattended-upgrades at Debian's time |
| The weekly notice on the reference | `UPDATE_CHECK` | absent, so `off`: no request |
| Immutable releases, without which a package install never installs tvgw by itself | the repository setting of decision 53 | off |

## 78. The uplink is judged passively from the gateway's own samples: no speed test, a warning at most, a plain line only when a person can act

*2026-11-08, from the competitive-gaps analysis of that day (its third gap, "is the gateway why the TV buffered?"). Built: `src/tvgw/uplink.py`, the brief's Uplink section and its JSON key `uplink`, the plain brief's `Uplink:` note, `tvgw uplink` and the verify check `uplink_quality`. The thresholds are starting values, read once against the reference gateway's record (below). No schema change.*

**Context.** Firewalla makes network quality a headline (a speed test of the internet connection, charts of latency and loss, a Wi-Fi test, "Network Health" in its app 1.69), and UniFi has WiFiman and its client experience score. tvgw's default uplink is Wi-Fi (`WAN_IF=wlan0`), so many gateways reach the router over the air, and when the TV buffers, its owner blames the gateway. Since [decision 48](#48-one-ledger-of-changes-one-hour-classifier-and-a-second-judge-that-only-watches) the sampler records, every 10 minutes, the uplink's Wi-Fi signal, its receive and send link rates and failed sends, the carrier changes of both ports and their byte counters (`gw_samples`). Only the hour classifier read them, to set hours aside as "Wi-Fi degraded"; no line told the owner.

**Decision.**

- **Passive only.** The verdict reads the samples the gateway already keeps. There is no speed test and no ping: either would contact a third party, and would load the line while the TV plays, which is when the question matters. So the verdict is about the gateway's own link to the router and the TV's cable to the gateway. It cannot see the internet connection beyond the router, loss or delay on the way to a streaming service, or the router's Wi-Fi to other devices, and no line says it can.
- **While the TV was in use.** Two consecutive samples of one boot, at most 1800 seconds apart (`activity.MAX_GAP`), make an interval. An interval counts when the TV port's counters label it streaming or awake ([passive TV activity](how-it-works/analyst-and-autonomy.md#passive-tv-activity)), labels read up to `MAX_GAP` either side of the window so the intervals at its edges keep their own, and when no label is known for it, so a gateway that cannot tell judges every interval and its plain line says "during the day" instead of "while the TV was on". This is the TV's own traffic, the same on any TV, not a reading only an LG gives.
- **Four findings, in this order** (`FINDINGS` in src/tvgw/uplink.py):
  - *dropped*: the uplink lost its link (its carrier) in an interval in use or right after one, at least `WAN_DROPS` times. The interval after counts too, because a long outage stops the TV's traffic, which would otherwise hide it;
  - *weak*: both readings of an interval under `WEAK_DBM`, for `WEAK_MINUTES` or more in the day;
  - *near its limit*: in one interval the TV's average download, the bytes the gateway sent out of `TV_IF`, over `NEAR_SHARE` of the Wi-Fi link rate. The link rate is the fastest of the interval's two receive and two send readings over `BASIC_RATE` (12 Mbit/s): a rate at or under it is likely that of a broadcast or management frame, the last one heard between a stream's bursts (6 Mbit/s is the 5 GHz basic rate, 6.5 HT MCS0), and says nothing of what the link carries. A link whose every reading is that slow is not judged by this finding. On a cable, over `WIRED_SHARE` of its speed, read from `/sys/class/net/<WAN_IF>/speed` when the brief or `tvgw uplink` runs, and used only for a window that ended at most `CABLE_FRESH` (1 day) before, so an older day is never judged by today's speed;
  - *TV cable dropped*: the TV port lost its link at least `TV_DROPS` times in an interval in use whose neighbours on both sides were in use too. Many TVs drop the link when switched off, and a restart or a software update that restarts the TV drops it once, so neither counts.

  A carrier change is a loss or a return of the link, so one drop is two changes, and an odd count ends on a loss not yet seen to return. A reboot starts the counters again, so the pair of samples across it is no interval. The failed sends are reported and not judged: nothing yet says what a normal count is.
- **The brief.** The engineering brief gains a section, `Uplink`, after "Changes in effect", with the figures and the verdict, and the JSON the key `uplink`; with `LEDGER=off` it says there are no samples. The plain brief gains one note line, `Uplink:`, after the other notes, on a day with a finding: what the gateway saw, then what to do if the TV buffered, each piece of advice once. The gateway cannot see a stream buffer, so the line never says one did. With every Wi-Fi reading in use at or above the weak line it points at the router, not at where the gateway stands: on 2026-11-02 the router gave the gateway a new address with the signal strong. A good day adds nothing, and the line carries no time of day, address or name.
- **A note, never the Action.** [Decision 58](#58-the-brief-has-a-plain-form-with-three-tiers-the-login-message-shows-its-status-and-action-and-tvgw-doctor-explains-every-check)'s `act` tier means a person is needed, and its transients stay off the Action line; one drop can be a transient (a mesh roaming the gateway, a router moving it to another band), the gateway cannot tell whether anything buffered, and these lines are new. So, as with [decision 66](#66-an-upload-budget-with-a-ceiling-set-once-count-only-and-the-names-of-a-tv-that-goes-online-while-off)'s `Uploads:` and `While off:` lines, the finding is a note: it never becomes the Action, never counts toward "N more things need a look", and `reports/action.txt` and the login message never carry it.
- **`tvgw uplink [--days N]`** prints the same figures, one row per local day, today so far first, for [the troubleshooting page](use/troubleshooting.md#if-the-tv-buffers-or-is-slow) to point at. It reads the store only.
- **A warning, never more.** `uplink_quality` in `tvgw verify` warns when at least `RECENT_WEAK` (3) of the last hour's samples read a signal under `WEAK_DBM`, or the uplink lost its link between two of them. `FAIL_AFTER` holds it at WARN for good, as [decision 58](#58-the-brief-has-a-plain-form-with-three-tiers-the-login-message-shows-its-status-and-action-and-tvgw-doctor-explains-every-check) holds every new check: a weak link is worth a look, and the gateway still passes the TV's traffic. Its detail holds counts and the line, never a reading. The doctor explains it and fixes nothing: the gateway changes neither its own Wi-Fi nor the router.
- **No setting.** The lines are constants in src/tvgw/uplink.py, the same on every gateway, so a brief means the same thing everywhere; the calibration below moves them for everyone.

**The starting lines, and why.**

| Constant | Value | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `WEAK_DBM`, `WEAK_MINUTES` | -72 dBm, 30 minutes | Cisco Meraki's voice guide asks for -67 dBm or better wherever a phone is used ([Wireless VoIP QoS Best Practices](https://documentation.meraki.com/MR/Deployment_Guides/Wireless_Voice_Deployment_Guide_(CVD)), read 2026-11-08). A video stream buffers seconds ahead and survives more than a call does, so the line sits 5 dB under that. Both readings of an interval and 30 minutes, so one bad reading never makes a day weak. |
| `NEAR_SHARE` | 0.5 | Wi-Fi delivers roughly half its link rate once its own overhead is paid: an estimate, not a measurement. On the reference the link rate reads 180 to 433 Mbit/s while a stream takes about 6 ([decision 48](#48-one-ledger-of-changes-one-hour-classifier-and-a-second-judge-that-only-watches)'s note), so it cannot fire there. |
| `BASIC_RATE` | 12 Mbit/s | The reference's receive rate read 6.0 or 6.5 Mbit/s on three of its eight days (below), the rates of frames heard between a stream's bursts. Without the floor, a 3.6 Mbit/s stream between two such readings read as more than half the link. |
| `WIRED_SHARE` | 0.8 | A cable delivers close to its speed. A cable that negotiated 10 or 100 Mbit/s, often a damaged one, is what this finds. |
| `WAN_DROPS` | 1 | Each drop interrupts what the TV is playing, and one on 2026-11-02 at 19:46 gave the gateway a new address and cut five of the TV's connections ([decision 48](#48-one-ledger-of-changes-one-hour-classifier-and-a-second-judge-that-only-watches)'s note). |
| `TV_DROPS` | 2 | A restart, or a software update that restarts the TV, drops its link once. |

**The calibration, 2026-11-08.** Two read-only queries ran on the reference gateway over its whole record of samples, 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-08: per day, the weakest and mean signal, the samples under -72 dBm, the slowest receive rate and the carrier changes of both ports (`tvgw sql "select date(t,'unixepoch','localtime') d, count(*), min(wlan_signal), round(avg(wlan_signal),1), sum(wlan_signal < -72), min(wlan_rx_mbps), max(wan_cc)-min(wan_cc), max(tv_cc)-min(tv_cc) from gw_samples group by d order by d desc limit 21"`), and the largest share of the link the TV's download took in any interval (a `with ... lag()` query over the same table). The signal read -31 to -34 dBm every day, with no sample under the line. The slowest receive rate was 6.0 or 6.5 Mbit/s on 2026-11-03, 11-04 and 11-06, which is why `BASIC_RATE` exists, and 58.5 to 200 Mbit/s on the other days. No interval took more than 0.072 of the link, against a TV peak of 3.6 to 28.7 Mbit/s a day. The uplink changed its carrier twice, one drop, on 2026-11-02 (the router's new address) and on 2026-11-07, and the TV port twice, one drop, on 2026-11-07. So on this record only `dropped` can fire, on those two days, and only if the TV was in use around the drop; the lines stay as they are. Whether the owner saw a stream stop then is not recorded. A line that later fires on a day the owner saw no buffering moves until it does not, with a dated note here. After the deploy, `tvgw uplink --days 21` prints the verdicts themselves.

**Consequences.** Nothing refuses traffic, nothing new is sent, and nothing is stored: the verdict is computed from the samples when a brief or `tvgw uplink` runs. The plain brief gains a note on a day with a finding; its Action line, `reports/action.txt` and the login message are what they were. The JSON key `uplink` is not in the analyst's pack. A gateway with `LEDGER=off` keeps no samples and gets "no samples" everywhere. A cable's speed is the one read now, so a day that ended more than a day ago is judged on a cable by its drops alone. The samples hold no temperature or throttling of the gateway's own processor yet; `uplink.py` marks where a throttled interval in use would join these findings.

## 79. A pause lifts only the shared tracker lists, for at most an hour, through the plan, ends on its own two ways, and then names what the lists were stopping

*2026-11-08, from the competitive-gap analysis (gap 4, brief G4). Built the same day. Nothing changes on a gateway until its owner runs `sudo tvgw pause` and answers yes; it exists only if the owner accepts a bounded, timed lowering of protection.*

**Context.** When an app breaks behind a Pi-hole or an AdGuard Home, the first thing its owner does is switch blocking off for a few minutes: Pi-hole's timed disable (`POST /api/dns/blocking` with a timer), AdGuard Home's timed pause of protection (since v0.107.28), Firewalla's pause of a rule "for" or "until". It answers "is it the filter at all?" in one step and comes back on its own. tvgw had `tvgw refused` ([decision 59](#59-site-rules-etctvgwlocalrules-written-by-tvgw-allow-and-tvgw-block-applied-after-the-profile-and-before-machine-rules-under-the-same-lint)'s note), which names the likely cause from the refusals of the last minutes, but cannot answer "would the app work without the lists?", and the only undo was per name. AdGuard Home's own switch (`/control/protection`) would also lift the URL filter's rewrites, the withheld record types and the must-allow exceptions, and an owner's page change is drift the plan undoes ([decision 6](#6-adguard-home-is-configured-as-code)).

**Decision.**

- **What a pause lifts.** Only the profile's shared tracker lists: every `[adguard] filters` entry whose `role` is `tracking`, the default (on `lg-webos`, HaGeZi LG webOS and HaGeZi Pro; on `generic-tv`, HaGeZi Pro). Everything else stays: the profile's own blocks, the owner's and the machine's rules, the must-allow exceptions, the URL filter's rewrites, the withheld HTTPS and SVCB records, every firewall wall (DoT and DoQ, the DoH address set, QUIC, LG's ad-tech ports), the TV's isolation from the home, and the list of encrypted-DNS servers, which each profile marks `role = "bypass"` by its entry, not by its name. That list closes a way around the filter rather than a tracker, so a pause that lifted it would let an app's own resolver take the TV's lookups past every rule. The general profile blocks no name of its own, so there a pause lifts every name block; the command says so before it asks.
- **Through the plan.** The pause is one row in the store's `meta` (`pause`: start, planned end, minutes, the lists, and once over when and how it ended). `adguardcfg.desired()` reads it and `plan()` renders the paused lists `enabled: false`, every other key byte for byte as without the pause, so every apply while it is in force keeps it (the owner's, a deploy's, a machine rule's) and every apply after its end takes it away. `desired()` drops the bypass list from any pause, whatever the row says. The apply goes through the restart guard ([decision 46](#46-the-blocklists-are-watched-and-adguard-changes-only-on-purpose)): with the upstreams down a start pauses nothing and exits 3.
- **At most 60 minutes, 10 by default, root, after a question.** A pause is a test, not a way to live without the lists: longer, "until tomorrow" or on a schedule is not offered. `sudo tvgw pause [MINUTES]` says what stays and what goes for this TV's profile and asks `[y/N]` (`--yes` skips it; without a terminal and without `--yes` nothing changes). A second pause while one is in force is refused with its end time; so is one while a pause past its end is not yet over.
- **Two independent ways back.** A transient systemd timer (`systemd-run --on-active`, unit `tvgw-pause-end`) runs `tvgw pause --end --timer` at the end, and a start that cannot set it pauses nothing. The hourly check (`tvgw autoblock check`, which may change AdGuard already) first ends a pause it finds past its end, whatever happened to the timer: a reboot that forgot it, a failure, an upstream outage that outlasted its retries. Not `tvgw verify`, which only checks. `sudo tvgw pause --end` ends early. The ends run under the autonomy's lock as `tvgw allow` does, but an end does not wait on a judgement for long: the timer's waits 15 minutes for an armed canary (11 at most), the owner's and the hourly check's 30 seconds and 2 minutes, then it goes ahead without the lock and says so. With the upstreams down an end leaves the pause in force and is tried again: the timer every 5 minutes for 2 hours, the hourly check every hour after that. While the upstreams are down the TV gets only what AdGuard has cached, so the cost of the lists staying off is small, and the restart would empty that cache for nothing.
- **The answer at the end.** When a pause ends, by any of the three, the command (or the unit's journal) prints, and `sudo tvgw pause --report` prints again later, the names the TV resolved during the pause that a list it paused blocks again now: read from AdGuard's query log for the pause's window as `tvgw refused` reads it (its file back to the store's newest lookup, and its API's pages), asked of check_host with the lists back (at most 200 distinct names, those the log does not show answered before the pause first), ranked as `tvgw refused` ranks its refusals (a name the profile needs, then one never refused before, then by lookups, known trackers last), each with its list and company, and the fix for the first: "If the app worked during the pause, the likely cause is api.example.net (HaGeZi Pro): sudo tvgw allow api.example.net". With nothing the lists block reached it says they were not the cause and sends the owner to `sudo tvgw refused` right after the app fails. Nothing is allowed for him: he runs `tvgw allow` himself. The timer and the hourly check run without a terminal, so where tvgw keeps no AdGuard password (`/etc/tvgw/adguard-admin`), as on the reference gateway, their report says how many names there were and that `sudo tvgw pause --report` at a terminal names them.
- **Recorded where the owner reads.** Each start and end is an `owner_pause` line in the change ledger, class A, with the minutes and how it ended; the start's line spans the planned end, so the hour classifier counts every hour a pause touched as confounded. The engineering brief's Rule changes lists each pause as a row, and the plain brief's New line says "the shared blocklists were paused for 10 minutes at 20:14". `tvgw verify`'s `list_sentinels` reports a paused list's sentinel as `paused until HH:MM` at WARN, never FAIL, and one past its end not yet over as a WARN that says what ends it; the doctor explains the first and offers `tvgw pause --end` for the second. A lookup answered while a pause had the lists off counts neither as the TV using a name a list then blocks (the list red line of decision 46) nor as a list no longer covering it. `tvgw adguard diff` names a pause in force instead of reporting its lists as drift. `tvgw pause --status` (adm) says whether a pause is in force and until when.

**Consequences.**

- The privacy cost, said plainly: for the minutes of a pause the TV reaches every tracker that only the shared lists block, and on the general profile every tracker at all. What the TV sent them in those minutes is sent. The TV keeps the answers it got for their time to live after the lists come back, so a tracker it resolved during the pause stays reachable for a few minutes more; the profile's own blocks, which stay, cover a vendor's own collection on the vendor profiles.
- Each pause restarts AdGuard Home twice, and each restart empties the cache the TV lives on; the namespace tier times both and writes the estimate to its artifacts. Hours with a pause are confounded for the judges.
- Names the TV reached during a pause are in the record as answered: the brief's New line may name a new service it reached only then, and the analyst's pack sees them allowed.
- With `LEDGER=off` no `owner_pause` line is loaded, so neither brief names the pause and the list section cannot leave its minutes out.
- A profile's `[adguard] filters` entry with a key other than `name`, `url`, `sentinel` and `role` is refused, as other tables refuse unknown keys: a misspelt `role` would leave a bypass list pausable.

## 80. Threat and newly-registered-domain lists are downloaded and matched on the gateway, counted only; a red line when the filter answered a known-bad name

*2026-11-08, from the competitive-gaps report's brief G5 (schema 16), revised the same day after an independent review. Count-only: nothing new refuses the TV anything, and no name the TV looked up leaves the gateway.*

**Context.** Firewalla (Active Protect; from app 1.69, blocking of domains registered in the last 14 days and of lookalike domains), NextDNS (threat intelligence, newly registered domains), Control D (malware filters) and UniFi CyberSecure (Proofpoint signatures, Cloudflare categories) all tell their owners when a device talks to malware or botnet servers. tvgw subscribed privacy lists only. For TVs this is no longer theoretical: the FBI warned in 2025 that BADBOX 2.0 runs on millions of uncertified Android TV boxes and projectors and turns homes into residential proxies, and Google took its operators to court in January 2026 ([BleepingComputer](https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/fbi-badbox-20-android-malware-infects-millions-of-consumer-devices/), [Krebs on Security](https://krebsonsecurity.com/2026/01/who-operates-the-badbox-2-0-botnet/), [HUMAN Satori](https://www.humansecurity.com/learn/blog/satori-threat-intelligence-disruption-badbox-2-0/), [Cloud Security Alliance](https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-badbox2-kimwolf-iot-supply-chain-systemic/), read 2026-11-08). Such a box gets the general profile ([decision 62](#62-a-tv-gets-a-vendor-profile-only-on-evidence-or-its-owners-answer-otherwise-the-general-profile-which-isolates-records-briefs-and-blocks-only-shared-lists)), which would not notice. The owner's rule decides the shape: new blocking counts first ([decision 20](#20-the-gateway-blocks-on-its-own-under-three-watches) and the two-week rule of [decision 17](#17-strict_ports-stays-off-until-two-clean-weeks)). The review that revised this decision found that a wrong or poisoned list would have put "the TV may be infected" on the Action line every viewing day, that a sinkholed name counted as let through, and that the default lists wrote about 86 MB to the card a day for a tag and a section.

**Decision.**

- **Matched here, never asked about.** With `THREAT_LISTS=count` the gateway downloads whole lists and matches the TV's names against its own copies (`src/tvgw/threats.py`). No name is sent anywhere. A per-lookup reputation service (AdGuard Home's Safe Browsing, Google Safe Browsing) is rejected: every name the TV looks up would leave the gateway. `THREAT_LISTS` absent or `off` downloads nothing and matches nothing, and an update with `off` removes the copies an earlier one made.
- **The lists.** `THREAT_LIST_URLS`, by default HaGeZi's Threat Intelligence Feeds in their mini form alone ([github.com/hagezi/dns-blocklists](https://github.com/hagezi/dns-blocklists), GPL-3.0, rebuilt daily; `tif.mini.txt`, 188,782 names, 3,979,770 bytes on 2026-11-08), the `tif` kind. HaGeZi's bands of newly registered domains (`nrd7.txt`, registered 1 to 7 days before, 2,706,800 names, 52,393,869 bytes; `nrd14-8.txt`, 8 to 14 days before, 2,531,486 names, 49,746,961 bytes), the `nrd` kind, are the owner's to add: they feed only a tag and a section, and cost 25 times the threat list's download. Their copies live in `/run/tvgw-threat`, a tmpfs the unit keeps between runs until the next boot, so they never reach the card; the timer's run at boot fetches them again. Lists are fetched at run time only and never copied into the repository; the tests use made-up lists (as for the profiles' lists, [decision 32](#32-a-vendor-profile-without-hardware-is-dns-only-cited-and-subscribed)). No public list of BADBOX 2.0's servers was found, so the threat intelligence feeds stand in for one.
- **The download** (`threats.update()`, `sudo tvgw threats --update`, run once a day at a random time in its first hour and 10 minutes after each boot by `tvgw-threat-update.timer`, before the 03:20 run that matches the day before). curl as `tvgw-doh-update` runs it: HTTPS only, redirects included, `--max-filesize` and `--max-time`; the size is counted again as the lines arrive (`MAX_BYTES`, 100 MiB, `FETCH_SECONDS`, 600 s). Each list is parsed as it arrives into a staged file beside its copy and renamed over it only when it holds at least `MIN_ENTRIES` (1,000) names, at least `DECLARED_SHARE` (90%) of the count its own header declares, and at least `SHRINK` (half) of the copy it replaces; a list whose parse hashes as its copy does leaves the copy as it is. Otherwise the old copy stays, the manifest records the error, and the unit fails, so the day shows in `systemctl --failed`. The unit holds no capability and writes only `/var/lib/tvgw/threat` (its `StateDirectory`, never the store, the rules or the pairing key beside it) and its tmpfs, under `ProtectSystem=strict`, a system-call filter, `PrivateDevices`, `ProtectProc=invisible`, `RestrictNamespaces`, `LockPersonality` and `MemoryMax=128M`.
- **The formats, and what each entry covers,** as AdGuard Home reads them: adblock `||name^` (with no modifier but `$important`), plain domains and dnsmasq lines cover the name and every name under it; `*.name` the names under it only; a hosts-file line its name only; an exception `@@||name^` takes the name and the names under it back out of that list. Comments, cosmetic rules, regular expressions, addresses, a whole top-level domain and a public suffix (`names.PUBLIC_SUFFIXES`: `co.uk`, `github.io`, a CDN's shared domain, which name no one tenant) are skipped; a Unicode name is kept in punycode. The parse of the three HaGeZi lists gave exactly the counts their headers declare.
- **Memory, measured.** Matching puts the TV's names of one day, with their parent domains, into one dictionary, and streams each copy past it a line at a time, so memory follows the TV's names, never a list's length. Measured on 2026-11-08 on the build workstation (an Apple-silicon Mac under a load of about 30), with the three real lists in a scratch directory and the code as revised: parsing them took 6.1 s at a peak of 26.2 MB resident; matching 3,000 names against their 5,427,068 entries took 0.43 s at 28.6 MB, where Python alone takes 16 MB. A Raspberry Pi 5 runs Python about two to three times slower, so about 18 s and 1.3 s, at the same memory, well inside the 150 MB budget. A unit test holds the matcher's peak flat while the list grows a hundredfold.
- **Answered means it reached a server.** A lookup counts as answered only when the filter did not refuse it and its answer holds a public address: an answer of `0.0.0.0`, an error with no address (NXDOMAIN, SERVFAIL) and the gateway's own rewrite never reached the server a list names, so a sinkholed or dead name is never "let through".
- **Matched once a day, again when a newer list comes.** The hourly ingest's `threats` step matches each local day `SETTLE` (3 hours) after it ends, when `tvgw-analyze` writes the day's file, up to `BACKFILL_DAYS` (7) days back. A day matched against a copy fetched before the day ended (the night's download failed) is matched again when a newer copy comes, and so is a day matched before a list's copy arrived (after a reboot). The day's distinct internet names, allowed and refused, from the `dns` table, become rows of `threat_hits` (schema 16): day, name, list, the entry that matched (the most specific), the list's label, the day of its copy and a newly-registered band, the lookups, how many were answered, the first and last time, and a doubt. The days matched are kept in `meta` (`threat_matched`), so a day matched with no hit reads apart from one never matched. `sudo tvgw threats --match DAY` matches a day on demand; a day not over is not marked, so the settled match still runs.
- **A doubt is recorded with each hit** (`threats.doubt_of()`): why the hit is more likely the list's error than the TV's, the first that holds: a must-allow host; a name in one of the profile's expected categories (on `lg-webos`, Streaming, Infra, LG core and FAST: a list that gained `||googlevideo.com^` would otherwise name every viewing day); a zone the profile never blocks at its apex; a lookup answered under an allow rule (`@@`, which is how AdGuard answers a name its own lists hold); or a name the TV looked up on at least `FAMILIAR_DAYS` (8) of the `FAMILIAR_SPAN` (14) days before.
- **Two tiers.** A threat-list name the filter answered, with no doubt, is a red line of the `act` tier, `known-bad name answered:`, one per name, up to `MAX_NAMED` (3) in a window.
