The plain Action line says what it means and what to do at the TV without the name, so `action.txt` and the login message never carry one; a `Known-bad:` line after it names the name, the lookups and the list. A name with a doubt, and every name when more than three without one come at once, make one red line of the `doctor` tier, `threat list may be wrong:`, which the plain brief words as the list's likely error and sends to `sudo tvgw doctor`; the doctor lists each name with why the list is doubted, how to stop it (another list, or `off`) and a copy a week old (`threats.doctor_notes()`). A refused name stays in the section.
- **Where it shows.** The engineering brief has two sections: Known-bad names, the threat lists' hits, and Newly registered names, kept apart since a new domain is not a known-bad one. The plain New line marks a new service on a newly-registered list by the day of the list's copy and its band (`new.example, registered in the 7 days before 7 November`). `tvgw check NAME` adds `On a threat list: yes (HaGeZi TIF, copy of <day>)` and its newly-registered twin, read from the copies. `tvgw threats [SINCE]` lists the rows.
- **The analyst's pack leaves both red lines out.** A model shown them could propose the block this decision defers; the names themselves reach the pack as they did, under New hosts and the rest.
- **Probation later, not now.** What would justify blocking: two weeks of these counts on the reference gateway with every false hit named (a doubt, or a name the owner recognizes as wanted), then blocks of threat-list names only, one rule at a time, through decision 20's machinery (the gates, the trial, the canary and the automatic undo), never a list subscribed in AdGuard Home. A list of hundreds of thousands of names that changes daily would block a working service overnight with no trial, and AdGuard's lists change only on purpose ([decision 46](#46-the-blocklists-are-watched-and-adguard-changes-only-on-purpose)). Newly registered domains would stay counted: a streaming service's new domain is new too. No setting in this decision blocks anything.
- **Not built.** Lookalike-domain detection, traffic signatures (an IDS), and a BADBOX-specific list, for the reasons above.

**Consequences.**

- What it cannot see: a server the TV reaches without looking its name up (the DNS gate counts those), and a name a list has not caught yet; a newer copy matches the last 7 days again only when theirs was fetched before they ended, so an older day needs `--match`. Such lists are sometimes wrong, and the red line says so.
- The familiarity doubt has a cost: a TV that was infected and looked its server up most days for a week or more before the list named it, or before `THREAT_LISTS` was turned on, gets the doctor-tier line, not the act-tier one. It is never silent: the line, the section and the doctor name it.
- The download tells HaGeZi's host (jsDelivr's CDN by default) and the networks between that this gateway's address fetches the lists, once a day between 00:00 and 01:00 and after each boot. It carries nothing of the TV's ([what leaves the gateway](how-it-works/privacy-and-retention.md#the-threat-lists)).
- The default copy takes about 3.4 MB under `/var/lib/tvgw/threat/` (3,412,796 bytes on 2026-11-08), rewritten only on a day the list changed. With both newly-registered bands added, about 86 MB more live in memory, in the tmpfs, and about 102 MB more are downloaded a day.
- The rows hold names and times of use: `DB_DAYS` prunes them, and `tvgw forget` empties them with the days matched.
- On a gateway with `THREAT_LISTS=off` nothing changes but one line of `tvgw size` and a timer that runs and does nothing; the brief is what it was.

**Owner decisions, held behind settings.**

| Decision | Setting | Default |
|---|---|---|
| Whether new installs count against the lists (recommended for the general profile, where the risk is) | `THREAT_LISTS` in the settings template | `off`, the value a file without the key means |
| Whether the reference gateway counts | `THREAT_LISTS` | absent: `off` |
| Which lists, and whether to add the newly-registered bands (about 102 MB a day, kept in memory) | `THREAT_LIST_URLS` | HaGeZi's TIF mini alone |
| Whether a threat-list name is ever blocked | none: the probation path above, after two weeks of counts | count-only |

## 81. Who answers the TV's lookups is the owner's choice among checked operators that do not filter, changed all or nothing

*Decided 2026-11-08 with the competitive-gaps report (gap G6) and built the same day. Two owner decisions stay open, below.*

**Context.** Every name the filter lets through is looked up by one outside operator, which sees it with the home's address, or the VPN's on a gateway that has one. tvgw sent every lookup to Cloudflare by address ([design decision 9](#9-doh-upstreams-by-ip-address)). Changing that meant editing `DNS_UPSTREAMS`, a key of URLs, by hand; setup never asked, and nothing said who answers. Pi-hole's installer asks which upstream to use ([Pi-hole v6](https://pi-hole.net/blog/2025/02/18/introducing-pi-hole-v6/)), AdGuard Home takes any list of upstreams ([its configuration](https://github.com/AdguardTeam/AdGuardHome/wiki/Configuration), read 2026-11-08), UniFi offers a list of encrypted presets and Portmaster lets its user pick a secure resolver ([Portmaster](https://safing.io/)), as the competitive-gaps report of 2026-11-08 found. A typo in that one key is also the one edit that can take every lookup from the TV at once.

**Decision.**

- **A short list in code, of operators that do not filter.** `resolver.CANDIDATES` holds, per operator, a plain name, who runs it, its country, its DoH URLs by IPv4 address (no host name, as decision 9 requires), the same operator's plain addresses as the bootstrap (so nothing is asked of a second party), and one line of what its own privacy policy says it keeps, with the link and the day it was read. Only operators that need no account and do not filter qualify: tvgw is the only filter, so no upstream may block a must-allow host behind it. For an operator whose main address filters, its non-filtering address is the one listed. `resolver.PRESETS` are the candidates whose DoH answered on each address with a certificate valid for that address; a candidate not seen to do so is offered nowhere and cannot be set.
- **What each policy says, read 2026-11-08.** Cloudflare ([its resolver policy](https://developers.cloudflare.com/1.1.1.1/privacy/public-dns-resolver/), last updated 2026-05-06): no client address written to disk, a truncated one and the resolver logs deleted within 25 hours, except a limited sample without addresses from which it makes aggregates it may keep indefinitely; the logs open to APNIC without addresses; not used for ads. Quad9 ([its policy](https://quad9.net/privacy/policy/), version 1.1 of 2026-06-24, Swiss foundation): no client address recorded; counters by name, network prefix and region, which may be archived. AdGuard DNS ([its policy](https://adguard-dns.io/en/privacy.html), 2024-08-06, AdGuard Software Ltd, Cyprus; [its server list](https://adguard-dns.io/en/public-dns.html) gives the non-filtering addresses 94.140.14.140 and 94.140.14.141): no personal data processed for public DNS; an anonymous list of the names asked in the last 24 hours, and counters. Google ([Public DNS privacy](https://developers.google.com/speed/public-dns/privacy)): the client address with each query in temporary logs deleted within 24 to 48 hours, then a sample without the address but with a city or region, kept for good; not linked to other Google services except for security, not used for ads. Google also sends part of the client's address (EDNS Client Subnet) to the name servers it finds take it ([its ECS page](https://developers.google.com/speed/public-dns/docs/ecs)); AdGuard Home sends none (`dns.edns_client_subnet` off), so that part is the gateway's public address or the VPN's. `resolver.Preset.shares` carries it, and `list`, `show` and setup print it. Mullvad is not a candidate: its page says its public encrypted DNS was discontinued on 2026-11-02 ([Mullvad](https://mullvad.net/en/help/dns-over-https-and-dns-over-tls)).
- **How each was checked, once, on 2026-11-08** from the build machine, with Python's `ssl` and the system's authorities, the address itself as the name to verify (so the certificate must list the address), and an RFC 8484 POST of three questions: `example.com`, `doubleclick.net` (a public answer shows the address does not filter) and a made-up name. `tvgw resolver test NAME` repeats it from the gateway.

| Operator | Addresses | Certificate valid for the address | DoH answered | Offered |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cloudflare | `1.1.1.1`, `1.0.0.1` | yes, until 2026-12-21 | yes; `doubleclick.net` resolved | yes, the default |
| Quad9, unfiltered | `9.9.9.10`, `149.112.112.10` | not seen | no: the TLS handshake was closed before a certificate came, also outside the shell's sandbox | no, held |
| AdGuard DNS, non-filtering | `94.140.14.140`, `94.140.14.141` | yes, until 2027-05-06 | yes; `doubleclick.net` resolved | yes |
| Google Public DNS | `8.8.8.8`, `8.8.4.4` | yes, until 2026-12-30 | yes; `doubleclick.net` resolved | yes |

- **Quad9 is held, not dropped.** The build machine's network also refused plain DNS to every public resolver, so Quad9's failure there may be that network's and not Quad9's; Quad9's own pages give its encrypted service by name only (`dns10.quad9.net`). It is held, not dropped: once `tvgw resolver test quad9` passes on the gateway or a CI runner, offering it is setting its `checked` day.
- **`tvgw resolver`.** `list` (adm) prints the choices in plain words with the one answering now marked; `show` (adm) says who answers now, what it sees and keeps, the fallback when `DNS_FALLBACK` names one, and, run as root, whether AdGuard Home's own upstreams differ from the settings; `show --json` is the same for a page; `test NAME` (adm) asks each address of a choice from the gateway, changing nothing; `set NAME` and `set custom URL[,URL]` (root) change it. `custom` takes DoH by IPv4 address only and refuses a host name, another scheme, a loopback, link-local, multicast or reserved address, and `GW_IP`, which would make the filter ask itself.
- **All or nothing.** `set` takes the install lock, then the autonomy's lock, then reads the settings and the profile again (a setup or another `set` it waited for may have changed `GW_IP` or the upstreams), and reads AdGuard Home's own `dns.upstream_dns`: "already answers" means both the settings and AdGuard say so, and a target the settings name that AdGuard does not ask is applied, as a repair. For the whole change SIGHUP (a dropped SSH session) and SIGTERM raise as Ctrl-C does, and the handlers before are put back after. It asks each new address directly first (a made-up name under the profile's first must-allow host, over DoH with the certificate held to the address), and refuses with nothing changed when one does not answer. It writes `DNS_UPSTREAMS`, and for a preset `DNS_BOOTSTRAP`, through `config.save_conf` ([design decision 29](#29-the-settings-file-is-edited-in-place-and-refused-whole)), which refuses a file the next command could not load, such as a `DNS_FALLBACK` on the new operator's address. It applies AdGuard Home the way `tvgw allow` does, through the restart guard: with the current upstreams down it goes past the guard only when the new ones answer a direct test at that moment, and otherwise exits 3 and puts the settings file back byte for byte. Then it tests the new operator through AdGuard Home at `GW_IP`, where the TV asks: the restart guard's own probe, a made-up name under the first must-allow host, must come back `up`, every must-allow name must answer with an address (`verify.must_allow_check`, with its one retry), and each new address is asked directly once more, since with `DNS_FALLBACK` set AdGuard may have answered from the fallback. Any failure from the write on, an interrupt or a test that cannot run included, puts the settings file back byte for byte (read and compared as bytes, so CRLF lines survive) and applies AdGuard Home again from it without the restart guard, because the guard would see the failed new upstreams and defer, keeping the TV on them, and the restart has already emptied the cache. SIGINT, SIGHUP and SIGTERM are held off while that way back runs, so a second Ctrl-C arrives once it is done. The DoH answer must be a DNS message (`application/dns-message`) answering the question asked: id 0, the response bit, a standard query, the same name, type and class. A change that took is one `resolver_change` line in the change ledger, class A, with the preset's name or `custom` and never an address.
- **One question in setup.** "Who should answer the TV's lookups?" lists the offered operators with one plain line each, its policy's day and attribution in it, and, when the settings name the owner's own, "keep your own" as the last choice. The choice the settings hold is the default, so Enter, a re-run and an unattended run without `SETUP_RESOLVER` change nothing. `SETUP_RESOLVER=<name>` in the answers file picks one. `DNS_UPSTREAMS` in the answers is the owner's own: it is not asked about and is written with the other settings, whatever form `config.load` takes (a loopback DoH proxy, `tls://`, a host name), `SETUP_APPLY=no` included, as before the question existed; where the gateway can ask it (DoH by address), a failure is a warning, not a stop. Beside `SETUP_RESOLVER` it is written the same way, after the named choice's test. A new choice is asked from the gateway first: an attended run asks again, an unattended one stops with nothing written. Setup never writes it with the other settings: once the installer has run (or on an installed gateway whose only change it is), it makes the change as `set` does, all or nothing (`Setup._switch_resolver`), and a change that did not take is said last and exits 1. When the installer stopped, the answers say `SETUP_APPLY=no`, or the filter is not installed, the lookups stay where they were and setup names the command for later. Written before the installer, a choice AdGuard Home's readiness wait refused would stay in the settings while AdGuard kept the old one, and every later apply would try it again: two restarts and an empty cache each time.
- **The restart guard does not hold a broken config.** `adguardcfg.apply` goes on past a deferral only when both hold: the upstreams AdGuard asks are not the ones the plan names (compared as sets after `adguardcfg.same_upstreams` normalizes them: order, a scheme's default port, a trailing slash and `#` comment lines do not count; a missing list is empty), and every planned upstream answers a direct DoH test from the gateway right then (`adguardcfg.planned_answer`, `resolver.precheck`; an upstream that cannot be asked that way, a host name or `tls://`, counts as not answering). Otherwise the deferral holds as before. The guard keeps a working config's cache through an outage; when the settings name another operator and it answers, holding the restart would keep the TV on the failed one. The readiness wait and the restore still stand. So `sudo tvgw adguard apply` brings AdGuard Home to the settings as soon as their operator answers, and is the recovery a failed way back names. `set` passes its own direct test, so its exit 3 means the current upstreams are down and the new ones did not answer that test either.
- **Said where the owner looks.** The engineering brief's Coverage line names who answers ("lookups answered by Cloudflare", or "your own resolver", never an address, since the report's JSON may reach the analyst's pack); the privacy page has a section on it; `show --json` is ready for the About page the competitive-gaps report plans. It prints the addresses, so it is for the owner's own page and never for the analyst's pack, which the analyst builds from the store and which imports nothing of `resolver` (a unit test holds it).

**Consequences.**

- A wrong choice can no longer leave the TV without lookups: an address that does not answer is refused before anything is written, and one that fails through AdGuard Home is undone with AdGuard Home put back. A way back that fails is said with what to run.
- With `DNS_FALLBACK` set, the probe through AdGuard Home cannot tell the new operator from the fallback, since AdGuard asks the fallback when the upstreams fail; the direct asks before the write and after the apply are what show the new operator answers, and `show` says so when a fallback is set.
- The direct ask sends the new operator a made-up name under the TV vendor's name a few seconds before it answers everything; it learns the vendor no earlier than it would.
- `set custom` keeps `DNS_BOOTSTRAP` as it is. AdGuard Home asks the bootstrap only to look up an upstream given by name ([its configuration](https://github.com/AdguardTeam/AdGuardHome/wiki/Configuration), read 2026-11-08), and every upstream here is an address.
- A deferral of `tvgw adguard apply`, the hourly check, `tvgw allow` or a deploy no longer holds when AdGuard asks other upstreams than the settings name and those answer a direct test; in an outage of the internet or the tunnel they do not, and the deferral holds as before, the optimistic cache kept. The direct test costs up to `resolver.DOH_TIMEOUT` (8 seconds) once the guard has deferred with the lists differing. Left: an operator that answers the direct test and then fails the readiness wait costs one restart and the restore's second, as any apply that fails its wait does.
- A power cut between the file going back and AdGuard going back leaves the settings old and AdGuard on the new operator; the next apply of any kind, or `sudo tvgw adguard apply`, brings AdGuard to the settings, and `tvgw resolver show` (root) says when the two differ.
- A policy can change without notice; each line carries the day it was read.
- Out of scope: operators that filter, need an account or a profile ID (NextDNS, Control D), DoT or DoQ upstreams, several operators at once (`upstream_mode` stays `load_balance`), a resolver on the gateway itself, and ranking operators beyond what each publishes.

**Owner decisions.** Whether setup asks the question: built so, with the default kept, so a gateway that never answers it behaves as before. The final list: built with Cloudflare, AdGuard DNS non-filtering and Google, and Quad9 held until its check passes.

## 82. Each rule and list is counted from the store; an allow unused for 30 days is named with the command that removes it, never removed

*2026-11-08, from the competitive-gap analysis's brief G7 ("what your own rules did, and which allows are holes you no longer need"). Built: counts per owner's rule in `tvgw rules --local` (with `--since` and `--json`), the refusals by list as the last section of `tvgw lists` (with `--since`), and the weekly report's Rule use section. Nothing is removed or changed on its own; no setting, no table, no column.*

**Context.** Firewalla's app 1.69 added rule usage statistics ([Residential Systems, 2026](https://www.residentialsystems.com/technology/connectivity/firewalla-releases-app-1-69-with-advanced-phishing-protection)); NextDNS ranks what each blocklist blocked, and "a query matching multiple blocklists counts once in each list's separate ranking" ([its API](https://nextdns.github.io/api/)); AdGuard Home's query log names the rule and the list behind every answer. tvgw has stored the rule and the list of every lookup since its first store (`dns.rule`, `dns.filter_id`), yet `tvgw rules --local` gave only each rule's day and reason. The owner could not see whether a block still did anything. More important for privacy, an allow written months ago to fix one app stays a hole in the protection after the app no longer needs it: the owner's allow carries `$important` and outranks every list's block and the machine's ([design decision 59](#59-site-rules-etctvgwlocalrules-written-by-tvgw-allow-and-tvgw-block-applied-after-the-profile-and-before-machine-rules-under-the-same-lint)).

**Decision.**

- **Counted from the store, by the rule's exact text, among the custom rules.** A block decided a lookup when the store's row is a refusal (`blocked = 1`) whose `rule` is the block's text; an allow decided one when AdGuard answered under it (`blocked = 0`, reason `NotFilteredAllowList`) and `rule` is the allow's text. The text compared is the file's line with its spaces stripped: the plan writes that line into `user_rules` as it is, and AdGuard names a custom rule by that text, case and modifier included, the comparison `tvgw check` makes to name a rule's source (`namecheck.source`); so `@@||news.example^$important` is not `@@||news.example^`. The store cuts `rule` at 2,000 characters; an owner's rule is far shorter. Only AdGuard's custom rules count (`filter_id` 0, or NULL for a row the log file wrote before 2026-11-07), so a list that carries the same text keeps its refusals. The store keeps the first rule AdGuard names for a lookup, the one that decided: a block whose names a list's rule decides first shows nothing, and still blocks if the list drops them. An allow counts every lookup it answered, whether or not a list would have refused it; what a rule would have done had it not been there is the probe's and the judge's question, not this one.
- **`tvgw rules --local`** adds, per rule, the lookups it decided in the last 7 and 30 days (`WINDOWS` in src/tvgw/rulestats.py), the distinct names in the 30 days, and the last time it decided one in the record. `--since SINCE` puts one window in place of the two, and `--json` prints everything as one object, for scripts and for a page later. It reads the `dns` rows of the owner's texts in one pass, since the store has no index on `rule`, and needs no more than `adm`.
- **An unused allow is named with the command that removes it, and nothing more.** An allow is named when it let nothing through in the 30 days before (`STALE_DAYS`), it was in force for all of them, and the record covers them: "Your allow of api.example.net let nothing through in 30 days. If no app needs it any more, remove it: sudo tvgw unallow api.example.net". In force means the plan takes it (the lint leaves it in) and every source that dates it puts it at least 30 days back: its comment line, by the end of its day, and the open row for its text in the rule history (`rules`). An allow either source makes younger, or neither dates, is not judged. The record covers the 30 days when its first ingest run is at least that old, `DB_DAYS` keeps at least 30 days, and its last run is within 2 hours of the window's end (`FRESH`, as `queries.STALE`); otherwise the output says why no allow is judged. `tvgw rules --local` and the weekly report name it; the plain brief does not, since this is a weekly task for whoever looks after the gateway. No code removes a rule: an allow is a hole only when no app needs it any more, which the owner alone knows. A TV unused for a month makes every allow look unused; the line says what the record saw.
- **The refusals by list** are the last section of `tvgw lists`, the list guard's command ([design decision 68](#68-a-lists-plain-exception-no-longer-lifts-the-profiles-red-line-blocks-the-lists-are-scanned-for-lines-that-act-on-the-tvs-rules-each-update-is-previewed-against-the-tvs-lookups-and-a-doh-list-refresh-that-drops-more-than-a-set-share-is-held-back)), after what the lists say about the TV's own rules; `--since SINCE` sets their window (default 7 days), its JSON carries them as `use` beside the guard's `state` and `previews`, and its exit status stays the guard's. They give each list AdGuard's settings switch on, by the name the profile gives its URL (`namecheck.list_names`), with the lookups it refused, the distinct names and its five most refused names with their companies. Then come the profile's own rules, the owner's and the machine's, always shown, and two rows shown only when they counted something: custom rules none of tvgw's files hold now (drift, or a rule taken out since), and AdGuard's own features or a refusal with no rule named. A custom rule's text goes to the first of the profile, the owner and the machine that holds it, as `adguardcfg.rule_groups` orders them. The owner's texts include each one the change ledger says an owner's command wrote, and the machine's every row of `auto_rules`, so a rule removed within the window keeps its row. Every refusal of the window lands in exactly one row, so the rows add up to the window's refusals. A list that refused nothing is said plainly, with `sudo tvgw verify` named where a sentinel checks that it is loaded, so the count and the sentinel check each other ([design decision 46](#46-the-blocklists-are-watched-and-adguard-changes-only-on-purpose)). AdGuard's settings file is AdGuard's own and may be readable by root alone: without it the lists go by AdGuard's number for them, a list that refused nothing cannot be shown, and the output says so. A name that is not an internet name is counted and never printed, as in `tvgw refused`.
- **The weekly report** ends with a Rule use section: the unused allows judged at the week's end, then the refusals by list over the week. A failure there is a report warning and costs no other section. The analyst's pack is built from `queries.window_report()` and does not carry it.

**Consequences.** The owner sees, per rule and per list, what the filter did for the TV, and an allow that stopped mattering is named every week with the one command that closes it. Nothing refuses traffic and nothing changes on its own. The counts are only as good as AdGuard's naming, one rule per lookup. A forgotten allow of a name the TV still asks for counts as used although no list may block that name any more; telling that case apart needs AdGuard asked about the name without the allow, which is not built. The machine's own rules get one row here and no counts of their own: the hourly judge already judges each of them ([design decision 20](#20-the-gateway-blocks-on-its-own-under-three-watches)).

## 83. No job of any workflow runs on a gateway: a change reaches one only when its owner deploys it

*2026-11-11. Built the same day: the `hardware` job and its 30-minute schedule are gone from `ci.yml`, with `tools/wait_deploy_window.py`, its tests and `.github/actionlint.yaml`, and the unit test of the workflows refuses any job on a runner GitHub does not host.*

**Context.** Until 2026-11-11 the reference gateway ran a self-hosted GitHub Actions runner as the owner's login, which had unrestricted password-free `sudo`, for a public repository, and `ci.yml`'s `hardware` job deployed every push to `main` through it ([decision 26](#26-the-workflows-are-the-most-privileged-code-in-the-repository), [decision 63](#63-behavior-changing-deploys-to-the-reference-wait-for-a-quiet-window-decided-on-the-gateway-nothing-the-gateway-prints-where-others-can-read-it-carries-a-home-identifier-acceptance-that-needs-systemd-runs-in-a-container-tier)). The job's guard (a push, to `main`, of this repository) was written in the workflow file, which the pull request or the push supplies, so it held nothing back from that file's author: one approved pull request that edits a workflow, or one push made with a stolen token, would have run as root on the gateway, the machine that holds the TV's firewall, inside the home network. On 2026-11-10 the repository's fork pull-request approval was set to every outside contributor (`all_external_contributors`); that narrows the first route and leaves the second.

**Decision.**

- **No job of any workflow runs on a gateway.** On 2026-11-11 the runner was stopped and disabled, to be deregistered from the repository, which is the control that holds (`gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/actions/runners --jq .total_count` prints 0). `ci.yml` has three jobs: `test` (a push to `main` unless it touches only Markdown, `docs/**` or `mkdocs.yml`; every pull request; a manual run), `deb-build` (pull requests) and `mutation` (the weekly schedule and a manual run). Nothing in CI deploys. `tests/unit/test_supply_chain.py` refuses any job whose `runs-on` is not purely GitHub-hosted labels, whatever its `if:` says (`test_no_job_of_any_workflow_can_reach_a_runner_github_does_not_host`).
- **A change reaches a gateway only when its owner deploys it.** `make deploy PI=<host>`, from a workstation, rsyncs the tree to a staging copy over ssh and runs `deploy/sync-install.sh` there under the install lock; `make hardware PI=<host>` runs the hardware suite on the gateway under the same lock. Both are unchanged.
- **The person who deploys keeps the quiet window.** A commit that changes how the gateway treats the TV still carries `Deploy-window: tv-off`, and `make gate` still checks it (`tools/check_deploy_window.py`). Before deploying a range that holds such a commit, the owner asks the gateway `sudo tvgw tv quiet --wait 0`, which prints `deploy: now` and exits 0, or prints `deploy: deferred` and exits 1, never saying why. `make deploy` records the commit it deployed on the gateway once the install ends well (`make deployed` prints it), and `make window` hands that commit to `tools/check_deploy_window.py --deploy`, which answers `tv-off` or `plain` for the commits since, and `tv-off` for a range it cannot read. `DEPLOY_WINDOW` and `tvgw tv quiet` stay, for the person deploying and for `tvgw updates`.
- **Not built: a gateway that fetches `main` and deploys it itself.** It would be safe only if it deployed commits the owner signed, and only ones that descend from the commit deployed; 461 of the 465 commits on `main` were unsigned on 2026-11-11, so it waits for a later decision.

**Consequences.** A push to `main` deploys nothing; a change waits on `main` until its owner deploys it, and is checked on hardware only when the owner runs `make hardware`. Nothing writes `/var/lib/tvgw/deploy-deferred` any more; one an earlier run left means nothing and can be deleted. The hardware suite's output is no longer published in a public CI log: it stays on the owner's terminal, and `python3 -m tvgw.scrub --stdin` on the gateway scrubs it before it is shared. The boundary for the repository and its releases is still the owner's GitHub account: a stolen token can still push to `main`, but what it pushes reaches a gateway only through that gateway's owner. A fork that registers a runner on its own gateway takes the risk back, and the unit test fails on such a job ([the supply-chain page](project/supply-chain.md#the-reference-gateways-runner-what-it-can-and-cannot-be-trusted-with)).

## 84. An apply that ends with no filter loaded switches the TV's port off; what counts as an odd port is judged per protocol

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, from findings L4-3, L4-4, L4-12 and L4-15, each first reproduced on 0.4.0.*

**Context.** Four faults in `tvgw-apply`, found by reading it against its own promises.

- [Decision 5](#5-fail-closed) says a half-applied gateway leaves the TV dark, never unfiltered. That held because forwarding is switched on last, and so only where forwarding was off before the apply. On a machine where another program set `net.ipv4.ip_forward=1` (a sysctl.d file, a router how-to), a first apply that stopped early left the TV forwarded with no `inet tvgw` at all, and said nothing.
- On a kernel started without IPv6 (`ipv6.disable=1`) the write of `disable_ipv6` failed, which stopped every apply before its load: the TV stayed dark for good, with an error that named a `/proc` path.
- One rule over both protocols left `{ 80, 123, 443 }` alone, so TCP 123 and UDP 80 passed with no log and no count, under `STRICT_PORTS=yes` too. On the reference the TV had used neither in the whole record.
- Before an apply removes a hook, it gives the in-path filler a moment to release the answers it holds. The moment was ten polls, which is no bound (a poll takes as long as the machine is busy), and answers still held when the hook went were dropped without a word.

**Decision.**

- **One EXIT handler fails closed.** An apply that ends before its own load looks for `inet tvgw`. With none, it looks once more under the apply lock, then switches forwarding off on the TV's port as the settings name it (read from the file itself when the read stopped early) and on the port the last apply filtered, says so for each port it switched, and exits non-zero. With an earlier apply's filter loaded it changes nothing: the old rules hold. The test is the table, never the shell's status alone: bash 3.2 hands an EXIT trap a status of 0 after a fatal error such as settings that do not parse. For the same reason an apply that reaches none of its own ends has failed, whatever the status says. An apply whose own load went in does not look. The confirmation's abort mark ([decision 65](#65-a-test-client-stands-where-the-tv-stands-an-apply-is-probed-before-and-after-and-only-reports-what-it-would-undo)) is left by the same handler, since a second EXIT trap would replace the first.
- **No IPv6, nothing to switch off.** `disable_ipv6` is written only where the kernel has the switch, and `tvgw verify`'s `ipv6_off` passes on a kernel that has no IPv6 on any interface.
- **Odd ports per protocol.** The log rule and the count-or-refuse rule are each two rules: `tcp dport != { 80, 443 }` and `udp dport != { 123, 443 }`. Both rules of a pair carry the old comment, so `tvgw verify` sums them into the one counter the store already keeps, and `tvgw ports`, the brief and the walls read on unchanged.
- **The drain is bounded by the clock and says what it dropped.** Two to three seconds at most (the shell counts whole seconds), read without a fork for each poll; an empty queue costs no wait; answers still held at the limit are named in one line.

**Consequences.** A failed apply on a machine with forwarding left on now turns the TV dark and says why. `STRICT_PORTS=no` counts two ports it did not, and `yes` refuses them: a TV that uses TCP 123 or UDP 80 would show them under Unexpected ports before anyone switches the wall on. The log's ceiling is 6 lines a minute for each protocol. Not built here, each with its reason in the pass's records: a start limit on the filler (a day of six deploys would strand it), an emptiness test on the kept routes (a VPN uplink keeps them empty), and a pattern for `WAN_IF` (it belongs with the shared TV-side block). Loading rules in place, so that no hook is removed at all, is a later decision.

## 85. One address per name: the minting table, first as a replay over the record that changes nothing

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, invention N1, stage 0a. The numbers are the reference's record of the 14 days before.*

**Context.** A DNS filter decides which names the TV may look up. The firewall sees addresses. The address gate ([decision 18](#18-dns-gated-egress-starts-as-a-count-in-its-own-table)) and the name count ([decision 70](#70-each-tls-connection-is-checked-against-the-names-the-filter-answered-with-its-address-a-report-from-the-store-a-live-count-from-copies-and-nothing-refused)) close part of the gap, and both stop at the same wall: one address serves many names. On the reference, 11 in 100 of the TV's connections went to an address the filter had answered for two or more names that day, so the record cannot say which name they meant, and no rule could refuse one of those names and keep the other.

The TV learns every address from an answer the gateway hands it. If each answer carried an address made up for that one name at that one real address, from a range that exists nowhere else, the address the TV connects to would itself say which name it means. The gateway would translate it to the real address in the same step that forwards the packet. A connection to an address no answer gave would lead nowhere, by construction and not because a set was filled in time; one name could be cut off though it shares a server with a hundred others; and every connection would be named with no hello read. Home routers with a "fake-IP" mode already hand such addresses to TVs to steer traffic into a proxy. Using them to bind a filter's word, on a gateway whose device cannot be changed, is what would be new here.

That is a large change to what the TV is told, and the TV keeps what it is told. So the first question is one the record can answer with no packet touched: had the TV been held to such a table, what would it have been refused? Counted by hand on the reference before anything was built: of 9,079 connections to public addresses, none went to an address no answer had named; 2 went to an address whose newest answer was more than a day old (45 and 121 hours); 84 in 100 began within a minute of an answer naming the address; and of the 2,685 whose answer's own lifetime the record holds, 81 began more than a minute after that lifetime had passed.

**Decision.**

- **The table lands alone.** `src/tvgw/mint.py` keeps one minted address for each (name, real address) pair an answer gives. It opens no socket and reads no store; every method takes the time. Its promises are tested over every order of events a property test finds: a live pair has one address and an address one pair; a pair answered again keeps its address and lives `LIFETIME` from that answer; a name over its share (`PER_NAME`, 256) loses its own least recently answered ticket and nobody else's; the clock never goes back.
- **An address rests before another pair gets it, and a full pool refuses.** An address nobody has held goes first, then the one that has rested longest, once it has rested `REST`: a week, because the oldest answer the TV was seen to connect by was five days old. With none rested, the answer gets no ticket, and that is counted. A full pool costs a refusal, never another name's server.
- **The pool is `198.18.0.0/15`**, the range RFC 2544 keeps for benchmarking: no internet host answers from it and no home network is numbered from it. Addresses ending in .0 and .255 are not given out, which leaves 130,048. The reference made 1,487 pairs in 14 days, 478 on its busiest day, and no name had more than 82 addresses in a day.
- **A ticket lives a day** (`LIFETIME`, the gate's day), and the replay shows what a shorter one would cost.
- **The first user is `tvgw mint replay`**, which reads the store and changes nothing. It gives the table every address the stored answers gave the TV, at the time of the answer, and puts each connection the TV began in one class by what the table held then: one name's ticket; several names' tickets; listed by the profile as reached without a lookup; crowded out (an answer named it within a ticket's lifetime and the table still held no ticket, because the pool was full or the name was over its share); lapsed (the newest answer that named it is older than a ticket's lifetime); never (no answer the record holds named it); or not assessed (the record does not reach back a ticket's lifetime before the connection, or its beginning was not seen). Crowded out, lapsed and never are what a strict stage would refuse. Connections to the gateway, to addresses that are not public IPv4 addresses, and to port 53 are left out. It runs one table for each of four lifetimes (a minute, an hour, six hours, a day) in one pass, gives every table every answer the record holds up to the window's end, whatever the window's start, and prints counts only: no name and no address, in `--json` too.
- **Nothing else.** No setting, no unit, no rule in any table, no change to the store's schema, nothing the TV can notice. A live shadow beside the name count, and anything that translates or refuses, each need a decision of their own, and the replay's numbers on the install in question first.

**Consequences.** Any install can ask its own record what a strict stage would have refused before one exists. The dev extras gain `hypothesis`, for the table's property tests; it is a test tool and never reaches a gateway. Two things are known and left open for the stage that would first translate. A program on the TV could fill the pool by looking up 130,048 names of its own, after which new pairs get no ticket until addresses have rested: a share for each registrable domain, or a pool the TV's lookups cannot exhaust, has to come before anything is refused. And the TV's own memory: at every boundary between minted and real answers (a first switch-on, a stand-down, a reboot) it holds addresses from the other side.

## 86. The firewall is worked out for every class of packets and held to written promises

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, from findings L4-13 and L7-4.*

**Context.** `tvgw-apply` is shell that prints nftables rules. What those rules do was argued in comments and sampled: 112 probe rows in the namespace tier, each one packet, and unit tests that look for lines of text. A prototype that tried one packet from every combination of the values the rules name found, in under a minute, that TCP 123 and UDP 80 had passed with no log and no count for as long as the rule existed ([decision 84](#84-an-apply-that-ends-with-no-filter-loaded-switches-the-tvs-port-off-what-counts-as-an-odd-port-is-judged-per-protocol)). Sampling had not found it, and would not find the next one.

**Decision.**

- **A model of exactly what the apply emits** (`src/tvgw/fwcheck.py`). It reads the scripts the apply hands to nft: tables, base chains with hook and priority, sets, and every rule clause by clause. A packet travels as in the kernel: the ingress chains of its port, prerouting, then input when its destination is the gateway's own (after any `dnat`) or forward and postrouting when not. An accept ends one base chain and the next still runs; a drop or reject ends the packet; NAT chains see a connection's first packet only.
- **What it cannot follow, it refuses.** A clause it does not know stops it with the rule named, so the model cannot fall behind the script silently. So do an interface name with `*`, a protocol given by number, a connection state it has no class for, a table of another family, a hook it does not walk, a chain declared twice, and elements added to a set it takes for a daemon's.
- **What the rules cannot decide is followed both ways:** a rate limit, a set a daemon fills, a queue, and the like. A rule with no verdict never splits the walk on such a question; what it counts is marked possible.
- **One packet from every class, found by running.** A walk starts knowing nothing of the packet. When a rule asks for a field nobody fixed, the walk is run again for each value of that field that anything names, and for one more that nothing names, chosen so. A walk that ends without asking stands for every packet that shares what it did ask. Nothing is sampled: the classes are the ways through the rules. The reference settings give 61,723 of them from the rules' own values.
- **Thirty promises, restated and cited** (`src/tvgw/fwpolicy.py`). Each says in a sentence what must be true, names the packets it speaks of and what must hold of their end, and cites the decision it comes from. No value in the file is taken from the rules: the ports, the private ranges and LG's addresses are written out again, and the DoH and block lists are read from the files the apply was given. They cover what must never leave (the home network, encrypted DNS, the walls that are switched on), what must always pass (the web, the clock, a small ping, the firmware fallback, a connection already open), what the TV reaches on the gateway itself, and that every refusal of a TV packet is counted.
- **Every value a promise names is tried, whatever the rules name.** A wall that loses a port no longer names it, and a search over the rules' values alone would let a stand-in speak for it. Each test in a promise says which values it names, and the search tries them all: the reference settings then give 109,882 classes. A rule that drifts from a restated value is caught, and a test takes a port out of two walls and a range out of the private list to show it.
- **Two promises are first made here,** because no earlier decision says them: a packet of the TV's that belongs to no connection the gateway knows does not leave, and no port wall cuts a connection that is already open.
- **Nine promises between two settings,** of every packet on the TV's port: each of the five counting values, and the probe, changes no packet's end; each of the three walls, switched on, lets nothing through that it refused when off. The counting values are also switched on all at once behind every wall. The probe's own port must end a new connection where the TV's would ([decision 65](#65-a-test-client-stands-where-the-tv-stands-an-apply-is-probed-before-and-after-and-only-reports-what-it-would-undo)).
- **Held to the kernel.** For every row of the namespace tier's matrix, the model must end the row's packet with the row's verdict, tick the counter it names, and for a packet let through say rightly whether it reached the gateway or went beyond it. CI proves those rows on a real kernel, so a model that misreads nftables disagrees with a row.
- **The promises are tested too.** A firewall with no rules and one that only drops must between them break every promise. And on `inet tvgw` alone, without the guard behind it, every deciding rule of the five chains that settle what becomes of a TV packet is taken out in turn, on two gateways that between them let each rule decide something. A promise must then break. Two rules are listed as unnoticed, with the reason: they are counters, and the matrix holds each to its name.
- **Settings.** Nine renders that between them hold every pair of values of the ten switches, the matrix's five scenarios, the clock port closed and narrowed, and a TV of no known make behind strict ports.
- **A test, and nothing else.** No rule changes, nothing runs on a gateway, and the wheel gains two modules that only the tests import.

**What it found on its first runs.** Three things the rules do not keep. A small ping passes to an address in `block4.txt`, because `tv_out` accepts small pings before it reads the list. With `TV_PROBE=on`, a packet of the TV's for the probe's own port or address is dropped by one of three rules with no counter. And LG's firmware fallback (one address, TCP 8080) is let out whatever the profile, so a TV of another make reaches it past `STRICT_PORTS=yes`. All three are in the test as known holes, each no wider than what was found, and a test fails when any part of one is closed until its entry is narrowed or removed. Closing them changes what the firewall does, so they have a package and a quiet window of their own. A third thing is recorded and not a hole: with the clock port narrowed to looked-up servers ([decision 71](#71-the-port-walls-are-ready-to-switch-the-probe-checks-them-and-tvgw-refused-reads-the-firewall), not yet supported on a live gateway), the rule judges every packet, so a flow already open is refused once its server leaves the set.

**Consequences.** A clause added to `tvgw-apply` fails the unit suite until the model reads it. A rule change that breaks a promise fails with the class and the rule named, before any kernel is involved. The suite spends about 45 more seconds of processor time. Not built: a reader for `nft -j list ruleset`, the checker run on a gateway before a load (report mode first), and probes drawn from the model's classes in the namespace tier.

*Note (2026-11-13): the three holes are closed.* [Decision 87](#87-the-owners-blocks-are-read-first-the-tvs-packets-stay-out-of-the-probes-chains-and-the-firmware-fallback-is-lgs) changes the rules, and the checker's list of known holes is removed: every class of packets is now held to every promise.

## 87. The owner's blocks are read first, the TV's packets stay out of the probe's chains, and the firmware fallback is LG's

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, the three things [decision 86](#86-the-firewall-is-worked-out-for-every-class-of-packets-and-held-to-written-promises)'s checker found on its first runs.*

**Context.** The checker holds every class of packets to written promises, and three classes ended otherwise.

- A small ping passed to an address in `block4.txt`. `tv_out` accepted echo requests of 128 bytes or less before it read the list, so the owner's block held for every port and not for a ping, two a second.
- With `TV_PROBE=on`, a packet of the TV's routed to the probe's port, or aimed at the probe's address, was dropped by one of three rules that carry no counter: the probe's chains are copies made without counters, so that the probe moves nothing the brief reads. The packet went nowhere, and nothing recorded that it had been sent.
- LG's firmware fallback (`156.147.69.32`, TCP 8080) was accepted whatever the profile. A TV of another make has no business there, and with `STRICT_PORTS=yes` it was the one address such a TV still reached on an odd port.

**Decision.**

- **`block4` is read second in `tv_out`,** after the uplink and the home network and before every accept. An address the owner blocked is refused whatever is sent to it, and the refusal is counted as `manual IP blocks` whatever the protocol.
- **The TV's own packets no longer enter the probe's chains.** The jump for what leaves by the probe's port takes `iifname != "<TV_IF>"`, so a packet of the TV's routed there stays in the TV's chains. A packet of the TV's for the probe's address is sent to `tv_out` by a rule of its own, ahead of the drop that has no counter and ahead of the accept of connections already open: the address is in the TV's subnet, so `tv_out` refuses it, by the uplink's rule or the home network's, and counts it. No counter is added and none of the probe's is touched.
- **The fallback's accept is built for `PROFILE=lg-webos` only,** as the ad-tech wall is. For any other profile TCP 8080 to that address is a port like any other: counted as an unexpected port, and refused with `STRICT_PORTS=yes`.
- **The checker's list of known holes is removed.** Its three entries are gone with the holes, and the namespace tier's matrix gains a ping to the blocked address and loses the fallback row for the general profile.

**Consequences.** On an LG TV the ping is what changes for a blocked address; and whatever else was refused there by a later rule (DoT, QUIC, a tunnel, a large ping) is now refused by the list's own rule and counted as `manual IP blocks`. A TV on another profile that was using TCP 8080 to LG's address now shows it under unexpected ports. With the probe on, a packet on the TV's port that claimed an open connection and was routed to the probe's port or address used to be accepted by the probe's chain when its source was forged; the TV's own chains now refuse it. The change is to what the firewall does, so it waits for the quiet window. A gateway with LG's profile and the probe off renders, byte for byte, what it rendered before apart from the moved rule and its two lines of comment; one with another profile also loses the fallback's line.

## 88. The screen test: a random half of fixed slots, an exact rank test of each destination's uploads, and a first stage that plays nothing

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, invention N4, stage 1. The measurements behind the numbers are in [the screen test's research note](research/screen-test.md).*

**Context.** Screen recognition is looked for by name (the profile's ACR names), by port (LG's ad-tech ports) and by shape (the upload test: much sent, little returned, within an hour). A client under another name, on port 443, inside a connection that returns as much as it sends, passes all three. What such a client cannot hide is what it is for: it reports what is on the screen. And the gateway is plugged into one of the TV's inputs, so it can decide what is on the screen.

**Decision.**

- **The test draws the order.** A run is 20 slots, 45 seconds each for a client that reports about every 14 seconds and two minutes each for one that reports about once a minute. A random half of them would show a moving picture with sound; the rest would be black and silent. Each destination's value for a slot is what the TV sent it in the slot's seconds, a quarter or a half of a slot later, since an upload reports what was on the screen before it.
- **An exact test, counted and not modelled.** The statistic is the rank sum of the on slots, one-sided. If the TV would have sent the same whatever was played, the values are fixed and the on label fell on a random half of them, so how often chance ranks a half that high is counted over every way to choose the half, ties included. No baseline, no assumption that traffic is steady or that one second is independent of the next. Two readings of each series (the bytes, and the seconds with an upload in them), two lags, and Holm's correction over every destination, reading and lag: a run with nothing to find reports something in at most 1 run in 100.
- **What a "no" is worth is measured in the run.** An upload of LG's published shape, and one of Samsung's, is planted in each destination the TV kept sending to (in three slots or more) on the run's own slots, and the result says in how many it would have been caught. A destination sent to once is no place to hide an upload, and finding one planted there would say nothing of the run. A "no" always carries its last line: a TV that reports on a timer whatever is on the screen, as three vendors were measured to do, looks the same.
- **The one-second poll** (`src/tvgw/connpoll.py`). The flow log has one line for a connection when it ends. The poll lists the TV's IPv4 connections once a second and takes each one's counters from the listing before: payload and not bytes (52 bytes of header are taken off for a TCP packet and 28 for a UDP one, an estimate under which a second of bare acknowledgements reads as nothing), by the destination's /24 and port, so a client that moves among a provider's addresses stays one series. A connection is the kernel's own entry, told apart by the entry's id. What the gateway answered in a public address's place, as it answers every lookup, is left out. A listing that fails forgets nothing: the next one counts what was sent since the last that was read.
- **This stage plays nothing.** `sudo tvgw screen-test --dry-run` draws an order and plays it to nobody, polls for as long as a run takes, and scores the result as a real run's would be. Nothing was on the screen, so nothing can follow: it shows what the TV sends while it sits there, and where a hidden upload would have been caught. It keeps nothing, loads no rule and asks the TV nothing. Without `--dry-run` the command says that playing is not built. **A run that could not look is not a "no".** When the connection table cannot be listed or carries no counters, when more than 1 listing in 20 fails, when a tick falls 5 seconds behind its second, or when the TV sent nothing at all, the command says that nothing is concluded and exits 2.
- **Proven on a stand-in.** The unit suite plays the pattern to a stand-in that plays the TV's part and sees only its own screen: LG's client on a server of its own is found in at least 97 runs of 100 of fifteen minutes; Samsung's in at least 95 of 100 of forty-one; LG's inside the busiest connection in at least 36 of 40 of thirty-six minutes; a client slow to start and stop in at least 97 of 100, and in far fewer with slots too short for it. A TV with no recognition, and one that uploads on a timer, are reported in at most 4 runs of 400.

**Consequences.** There is a check for screen recognition that needs no name, no port, no ratio and no volume, and its false-alarm rate is a property of its design. The gateway gains one command that reads the connection table once a second for 15 or 41 minutes (20 slots, and the longest lag after the last); what that costs on the reference is the dry run's first measure. Not built: playing the pattern (the next stage, with the owner's word), the same run in the namespace tier, any stored result or brief line, and a run on a TV whose recognition is on, which cannot be the owner's.

## 89. Ad breaks are read from the lookups an ad makes: a report first, nothing stored, nothing refused

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, invention N3, stage 0.*

**Context.** The gateway cannot take an ad out of a stream: on most services the ad comes down the connection the show comes down. It had no way to count ads either. But an ad is measured. When one starts, the player calls the firms that count it, several within a second or two, and each call begins with a lookup. The filter refuses those names, so the lookups are in the record already.

**Decision.**

- **The label is a refused lookup of an advertising firm's name.** Which names are advertising firms', and which company each belongs to, comes from the companies database tvgw already pins: the rows it files under advertising are written to `src/tvgw/data/adfirms.txt` in the same run as `companies.txt`, under the same licence. Only refusals count. A name the filter answers is kept by the TV for as long as the answer says, so its lookups are no clock; and the general names the database also files under advertising, a search engine's among them, are answered and so never count.
- **Three firms within 10 seconds open a break; a label within 65 seconds of the last extends it; it ends 20 seconds after its last label,** since a lookup marks an ad's start and not its end. Firms are companies, not domains: one company's software asks several of its own domains at once, and that is no ad. The 65 seconds are longer than the minute the TV holds a refusal, because the ads of one break call many of the same firms and the TV does not ask a name again sooner. The design's simulation had 45; on the reference's record that split the same breaks into 42 pieces, 60 seconds apart. The rule is fixed before any break has been marked by hand, and a test pins it.
- **No break lasts ten minutes, and two things follow.** Some firms' names are asked whatever is on the screen, by an app's own measurement or by the TV. A name asked without a pause for longer than a break lasts is one of them: it is a timer when over seven tenths of its lookups come in stretches of more than ten minutes with no gap over 160 seconds. Such a name is no label and no other name's company. This is the test that knows the names that beat every minute or two for longer than a break lasts, however many they are and whoever is refused beside them. It asks for most of a name's lookups because one freak of a break that long must not make timers of an ad's firms for the whole window. The 160 seconds were set by measuring on the stand-in: 150 misses the names that beat every two and a half minutes, and 200 joins the breaks of a service that breaks every two minutes or so, on a TV that holds a refusal for ten seconds, into stretches that make timers of its own ad servers. The other thing: a stretch of labels that runs past ten minutes is not counted as a break. The report sets it aside, counts it nowhere else, and says how many such stretches there were and how long they ran.
- **A slower timer keeps a steady beat and comes alone.** A name asked every few minutes pauses as an ad's name does between two breaks, so it is no label when two other things hold together. It is steady: within each of its runs, seven tenths of the time lies in gaps no longer than three times the run's usual one, where an ad's name has a minute's gaps inside a break and many minutes between breaks. And it comes alone: under half of its lookups have two other firms refused within the same 10 seconds, where an ad's name is refused with the other firms of the ad. A run ends when the name is absent for 45 minutes, and a name is judged from 20 gaps that come to half an hour. Eight other tests were tried and measured first, on the tests' stand-in and on the reference's record, and each failed on one of them. A share of the five-minute bins of clock hours hid every timer of an evening that began at twenty to the hour. The same share within the times the TV said one app was in front broke where one session met the next, and where the TV gives hourly readings. Any count of bins takes the firms of a service that breaks every few minutes for timers, which is what the reference's own record holds: its break names are in seven tenths of the bins of their own runs. Steadiness without company took a name asked once a break, at breaks that come evenly, for a slow timer. Company counted within two seconds told a crowd of timers apart and lost the ads that call only three firms, whose names are refused seconds apart once the TV holds one of them (7% found on the stand-in, against 91%). Counting lookups an hour, the oldest test, called 16 names timers on the reference, 6 of them advertising firms' names that are none. And steady and alone as the only test, with every timer counted as company, failed where a few more names beat every minute: four of them gave the stand-in's slower timers company enough to make labels of them (36% found, and 29% of what was found were breaks), and with three it turned on how the beats fell. Leaving every timer found out of the others' company, round after round, cured a dozen slower timers and made timers, one after another, of the firms of an ad that calls only three (7% found, against 91%).
- **A break belongs to the app the TV said was in front** when it began, and an app's hours are the hours it was in front with the screen saver off.
- **It is a report.** `tvgw experiment adbreaks` prints, for each app, hours in front, breaks, breaks an hour and about how many minutes of ads. Beside every figure it says that there may be more breaks than it counts, that the minutes are an estimate, and that nothing has been checked against breaks marked by hand. An app with ten hours and no break is printed as one whose ads cannot be seen this way. It prints how long this TV waits before it asks a refused name again, which is what the rule rests on, and says so when that is much under a minute, where the minutes run long. It stores nothing, refuses nothing and names no lookup.

**Measured before it was deployed.** On the reference's 14 days, read-only and as counts: the TV asks a refused name again after 60 seconds (the shortest usual gap of each of 31 names; 15 of 31,369 gaps were shorter than 55 seconds). The report's own code found 25 breaks, every one while the app of one ad-supported service was in front, at 3.7 an hour, of 3 to 10 firms each and 141 seconds at the median, the longest 212 seconds, about 50 minutes in all; none in the hours of the four other apps, none with no app known, and no stretch past ten minutes. Two advertising firms' names are timers there: one of 255 gaps of about 70 seconds, with every one of its lookups in stretches of over ten minutes, and one of 54 gaps of about 500, never once in company. No other firm's name has a single lookup in such a stretch. The names of the breaks have a fifth of their time in their usual gap and nine lookups in ten in company. On a stand-in evening of the tests (`tests/unit/adbreak_synth.py`), which begins at any minute of an hour and has eight advertising firms' names on timers, the rule finds 97% of the breaks whose firms the TV itself calls, 98% of what it finds are breaks, the start is 0.07 seconds off at the median, and found minutes over shown are 1.03. With three more names on beats of about a minute, with sixteen more on beats of up to two and a half, or with three firms that keep one such beat, every figure is the same to the last digit. Where one evening in ten has breaks of twelve to fourteen minutes, no firm of theirs is taken for a timer. With a break every four to seven minutes it finds 98%, and where each ad calls only three firms, 91%. Those figures rest on the app's own ad server being a firm the filter refuses; where it is not, 79% are found. Where the service's own servers report half of the breaks, the rule finds 98% of the other half and almost nothing of the first. On a TV that does not hold a refusal at all, 98% are found and the minutes run 16% long; held for five minutes, 92%.

**Consequences.** `tvgw experiment` has a sixth report, and the package a second data file under CC BY-SA 4.0. `companies.snapshot()` and `companies.trouble()` take which file. The services whose ads call no firm from the TV print as "cannot be seen". What neither timer test knows is a limit, and the report marks only the part of it that runs past ten minutes. Three firms that keep one beat of a few minutes read as a break at each turn (on the stand-in, 17% of what the rule then finds are breaks). A dozen names on beats of three to five minutes are company to the slower timers: 35% found and 28% of what is found are breaks; with sixteen, no name is alone and the evening reads as one stretch of three hours, which is set aside. A name on a fast beat that runs only in spells shorter than ten minutes, as an app's own beacon does when the app is opened for a few minutes at a time, is neither kind: one carries breaks on, and four in step read as breaks (64% found, 57%). A refused ad call that the player retries for some minutes after a break carries the break on for as long. An ad server asked at the start of every short video is taken for a slower timer. And in a window of a day or two, one evening of breaks longer than ten minutes makes timers of their firms. The reference's record holds none of these. Not built: scoring the report against breaks marked by hand, which comes with the marking kit; a service's own ad-decision names, seen once on the reference during a break, whose rule waits for marked breaks; a detector on the video connection's rhythm; a kept meter by day; a line that says a break is on now. Nothing here mutes or skips anything.

## 90. `tvgw forget` keeps one total of each firewall counter and no timeline, one count for the clock port's guard, and nothing that a cursor or an open capture file held

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, package P06. It closes four ways the command kept what it said it deleted.*

**Context.** `tvgw forget` says it deletes every viewing-derived row and file and keeps the gateway. It kept `fw_counters` whole, as "the proof that it held". But `tvgw verify --record` stores a snapshot of every counter each ten minutes, and the counters grow only while the TV is on, the refusals as much as the accepts. In a test of three evenings, every watched hour could be read back from the refusal counters alone after a forget. The same table kept the TV's time servers by address. A cursor kept the first line of the change spool forget had deleted, and the journal's cursors the second of the TV's last sample. The capture file that was open stayed for `PCAP_DAYS`.

**Decision.**

- **One total of each series, at the time of the forget.** For each counter the rows of its newest snapshot stay, moved to the forget's time; every earlier snapshot goes. A total says that the rule held, and when nothing. The kernel's counters run on, so the next snapshot is read against that total, and what the firewall is reported to have done after the forget is what a store never forgotten reports, on the day of the forget and after. Two rules that share a comment keep both rows. A series whose newest snapshot is a day older than the newest of all is a rule the firewall no longer has; kept at the forget's time it would say the rule was there then, so it goes.
- **The time servers go whole, and the clock port's guard keeps one number.** The rows that name the TV's time servers, and the count of what it asked them, are deleted. tvgw-apply closes the clock port only when neither the kernel's list nor the record shows the TV using NTP ([decision 71](#71-the-port-walls-are-ready-to-switch-the-probe-checks-them-and-tvgw-refused-reads-the-firewall)), so forget first keeps how many requests the last two weeks held, as one number that stops vouching two weeks later, and `tvgw ports --ntp-seen` adds it. It holds no address and no time of a request, and the hourly ingest removes it when its two weeks are over. A request the TV made since the last snapshot is in the kernel's list alone, so forget reads that list too and keeps the larger of the two counts; the record is read to its newest snapshot, wherever the clock stands. The list is read last, when the rows have gone and just before the commit, and emptied as soon as the commit holds the count, since the next `verify --record` would otherwise write its addresses back: a request made while forget works on the store is then counted, and only one made in the moment between that read and the flush is not. A list it cannot read (not root, no such list) it leaves alone, and says so under "not reached"; with no store to keep the count in, it leaves it alone too.
- **Cursors.** The change spool's cursor is reset with the others. A journal cursor, which forget cannot reset without reading thirty days of journal again, is moved up to the forget's time: the ingest reads it the same way. A journal source with no cursor yet is given one there, or its first read would go thirty days back and leave a sample's second behind.
- **The open capture file is named and goes when it can.** Forget cannot delete the file the capture is writing, and it does not restart the capture. It records the file's name; the hourly `tvgw prune-pcaps` deletes it as soon as the capture has moved on and the ingest has read it, whatever `PCAP_DAYS` says, and the ingest drops its rows as before. The name goes with the forget's floor, seven days later.
- **The verify history names no time of a TV reading.** The history of the gateway's own checks is kept. One failing check said when the TV was last read, and the TV is read only while it is on, so a run of such failures marked the hours of use. That check no longer says the time, and forget rewrites the failures stored before without it.
- **A late snapshot is late evidence.** A `verify --record` that began before the forget writes below its floor after it. The hourly ingest now drops such rows of `fw_counters` with the rest.

**Consequences.** After a forget the count-only runs and the readiness of `tvgw ports` start their two weeks again, as every count that rests on forgotten rows already did. `tvgw refused`, whose window may begin before the forget, starts its count of the firewall's refusals at the totals the forget left. A reload of the firewall between the last snapshot and the forget is not marked in the record; a lower count is read as a reset either way. Not built here: emptying the gate's kernel sets, which hold addresses for a day and are copied nowhere, and whose emptying would count every connection to an address resolved before the forget as a miss in the evidence the in-path gate is judged by; restarting the capture; and asking AdGuard Home to clear its own query log and statistics, which stays in the list of what tvgw does not reach until it has been tested against AdGuard itself.

## 91. What the machine applies on its own is one rule for one name; a revert AdGuard did not take is owed; no rule stays without a record; and the pack holds no time of day

*2026-11-13. Pass 6, package P04. Autonomy (`ANALYST_APPLY`) is off by default and off on the reference; the pack is sent on every night that has something new.*

**Context.** Four findings of pass 6, each reproduced on the code as it stood and again by a second reader.

- **The gate judged the heading, and the rule could be wider.** The vetting passes a rule that acts on the name its proposal is headed with, and a zone above the name, a wildcard or a regex does. The gate then read the traffic of that one name. A proposal headed with a beacon and carrying `||example^` was applied, and took a streaming host under the same zone with it. Seven wider forms of seven passed both. Of the six blocks the reference's own proposer has written, two were wider than their heading.
- **A re-apply that failed was never tried again.** A revert takes the rule out of the file and records it. When AdGuard then did not take the file (another apply held its lock, it did not come back, the disk failed), the failure was written down and nothing retried it. With three such faults at each of the three places a revert is made, nine of nine left AdGuard refusing a name the record called reverted.
- **A rule could stay in the file with no record.** A run that dies between the apply and the record leaves one, and nothing would ever judge or revert it. The troubleshooting page told the owner to delete it by hand.
- **The pack said when.** Each traffic shape carried the first and last second its name was used, each gated connection the same, and each rule change the second it was made: 85 numbers inside the window in one night's pack on the reference. The page of what leaves the Pi says the pack holds no timeline.

**Decision.**

- **Narrow by construction.** `autoblock.gate` clears a block only when three things hold beside what it held before. The rule is `||name^`, with or without `$important`, for exactly the name in the heading. The name is one party's own public name: no top-level domain, no public suffix and no wildcard, under which the store holds nothing to read. And the reach of the rule holds nothing else: every name the store saw at or under the name in the last 30 days, or since the gate's window began where that is further back, is outside the profile's expected categories, the name itself among them, and their bytes down over the window, summed, and the largest of their median connections are within the limits a single name is held to (`autoblock.reach`). A connection is counted under its name whether or not its hello ended the name with the root's dot. The reach is an argument the gate cannot be called without. A block that fails one of the three is not judged unsafe: its verdict begins `for the owner`, it stays a proposal, and the name is remembered as judged. The vetting is unchanged, so the owner still sees a wider proposal as `ok` and can apply it.
- **Exactly the name, not "whatever the rule's target reaches".** Judging the reach under a rule for a zone above the name would be as safe in its evidence, and one of the reference's six stored blocks, a rule for the zone of a name with nothing else under it, would have cleared it. The exact form is kept because then the heading the owner reads, the name the second model is shown and the thing the rule does are one thing, and the property can be stated and tested in a line. What it costs is that a host whose first label changes with every device can be blocked on its own only by its exact name. That is weighed again with the decision to turn autonomy back on.
- **The name's own category counts.** A new name under a zone the profile calls the TV's own is in an expected category by its zone, and is not blocked unasked however much its traffic looks like a beacon's: a quiet service the TV needs has the same shape. Before, the gate never read the category.
- **The second model.** An app's author chooses its id, the TV reports it, and it was the one free text both models read. One that reads like an instruction is replaced in the second model's message.
- **Owed until applied.** A revert owes AdGuard a push from the moment its row is written, and stops owing it when an `apply` row says `pushed` (`autoblock.push_owed`). So a revert whose push failed, one whose push waited for the upstreams, and one whose run was stopped before the push, by a unit's timeout, a reboot or a power cut, are owed alike. The machine's own reverts write the row before the rule leaves the file; the owner's undo takes the rule out first and writes its row before the record's verdict, so stopped at any step it leaves either a row that owes or an active record whose rule is gone, which the next check reads as the owner's. A new block's `applied` row settles nothing: its apply was planned from the file as it stood before, and an undo made meanwhile is not in it. A `pushed` row has the same weakness and is written under the same rule: a push notes the newest row before it plans (`autoblock.newest_decision`), and when it lands, `pushed` is written only if no revert and no owed row came after that one (`autoblock.record_push`). `tvgw undo` waits for no lock of the autonomy's, so an undo can come while the hourly check's or the canary's apply waits for AdGuard; that apply then lands a plan that still holds the undone rule, and its `pushed` row would have settled the undo's debt. Unwritten, it leaves the undo owing, and the next check pushes again. What this does not close is a plan read in the instant between a judge's row and its rule leaving the file, by a run that is then stopped before its own push; reading AdGuard's rules back against the file would, and is not built. Every hourly check applies again until one takes, says so when it does, and fails its unit each hour until then, which is the signal; nothing bounds it, and where the failure is AdGuard not coming back, each hour's try restarts AdGuard and costs the TV its resolver for the seconds that takes. An apply that finds AdGuard's own lock held is tried again, six times in all and 5 seconds apart, by the hourly check, the canary and `tvgw undo` alike, so a revert that meets a deploy lands within the half minute. The pause had that retry already, and they now share it (`adguardcfg.retry_busy`). On a gateway that reverted a rule before this change, the first hourly check after the upgrade applies once more and records it.
- **No rule without a record.** The hourly check takes out of the machine's file any rule that no record on probation, untested or passed names, records it as reverted with the reason, and applies AdGuard. The file is the machine's own: a rule of the owner's belongs in the site rules.
- **No time of day in the pack.** The shapes lose their first and last start, which no code read. The gated connections go without theirs. A rule change says which of added and removed fell in the window. The red lines that name a time in the brief (Live Plus since, an agreement accepted at, the busiest hour of an ACR-shaped upload) name its day in the pack (`queries.red_lines(o, hours=False)`). The brief prints all of them as before. A test builds a pack and holds that no number in it is a second inside its window and that no red line states a time of day.

**Measured.** The other forms the vetting admits (a zone above the name, a wildcard, a regex, a record type, another name, a top-level domain, a public suffix, a heading that is itself a wildcard) are each refused, end to end as well, and a property test over names with wildcards and empty labels holds that whatever the gate clears is the one rule for one party's public name. The nine fault cases, and a run stopped in the middle of its push, each end with AdGuard holding the file's rules at the first check after AdGuard mends, and with one row saying so. A rule left by a killed run is out at the next check, and the check after changes nothing. The reference's six stored blocks, replayed read-only and as classes and counts: the four exact ones pass the three new conditions, and the two wider ones go to the owner.

**Consequences.** With autonomy on, fewer blocks apply by themselves: no rule for a zone, no new name in an expected category. The gate's margins gain what the rule refuses (`reach_*`). A line written by hand into the machine's file is gone within the hour. Cut after the second reading, each for its reason: a lock around `tvgw adguard apply`, which would stop a deploy that meets a canary; writing the record before the apply, which would count a failed attempt as a block; a check that AdGuard holds exactly the planned rules, which waits for the apply's read-back. Not built here: masking identifier-shaped labels in the names the pack carries. The rule first proposed for it would mask 136 of the 349 names the reference looked up in two weeks, 122 of them in expected categories, most of them ordinary host names; it needs its own design and is the next package.

Each item is listed once, with the setting that controls it in `/etc/tvgw/tvgw.conf` ([the settings file](reference/settings.md)) and the value it has until the owner chooses. "No setting" means the choice is a change of code, of the profile or of the Pi, not of the settings file. Nothing here changes on its own.*
